## F2-30 · The bilingual invariant is documented, sold, and enforced nowhere

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-30 |
| **Category** | Data integrity / product claim vs implementation / Arabic-first localisation |
| **Severity** | **P2 Medium** — no security exposure, but it silently voids the platform's primary market differentiator on a per-record basis |
| **Provenance** | The v1.0 audit's **`F-007`**, verified and sharpened during the 2026-07-25 reconciliation: `ChangeRequest.titleEn` is `nullable = false`, `titleAr` has neither `nullable = false` nor `@NotBlank`. Spec written 2026-07-26 when the item was scheduled — it was one of the F2-29…F2-35 rows the plan owed a spec for |
| **Current State** | `CLAUDE.md` house rule 7 states *"user-visible records carry `*_en` **and** `*_ar`; Arabic names are backend-mandatory"*. Measured by reflection over every `@Entity`: **574 `*_en`/`*_ar` field pairs, of which 157 are asymmetric** — the English side mandatory, the Arabic side free — and **zero entities in the platform carried a single `@NotBlank`**. Not one layer checked the invariant: not the entity, not the DTOs (`@NotBlank titleEn` sits beside a bare `titleAr` in `SupplierResilienceDtos` and `FourthPartyExposureDtos`), not a test |
| **Problem** | Three failures. **1. The claim is false per-record**: a change request, a board resolution, a BCP plan or a policy can be created English-only and nothing objects, so an Arabic-first tenant opens a governance record and finds nothing in Arabic. **2. It is unfalsifiable from the outside**: because nothing enforces it, "is the platform bilingual?" can only be answered by inspecting data, and the answer varies by row. **3. The gap tracks module age** — blank-Arabic rows by schema: `risk` 130, `sust` 74, `evidence` 49, `policy` 13, `privacy` 10, `bcm` 6. The newest modules conform only because their seed data happened to be bilingual, not because anything held them to it |
| **Root Cause** | The invariant was written as a *house rule for developers* rather than a constraint in the code, so it degraded exactly as an unenforced convention does — from the oldest module outward. `Risk.titleAr` is blank on **66 of 132 rows**, half the risk register of the platform's first module |
| **Business Impact** | Arabic-first bilingual operation is the platform's core differentiator in Saudi Arabia and the reason it wins against ServiceNow/Archer on localisation. A regulator, board member or auditor reading an Arabic UI and finding an English-only resolution body is a direct hit on that claim — and PDPL/NCA submissions are made in Arabic |
| **Technical Impact** | Small per field, but the enforcement mechanism had a hole that had to be fixed first — see *Prerequisite* below. No migration: bean validation, not a DB constraint, because a `NOT NULL` column would need a backfill and nothing can invent Arabic governance text |
| **UX Impact** | Positive where enforced: the write fails with a **400 whose `errors` map names the field**, which the frontend's `problemMessage()` already folds into a toast. Negative if enforced carelessly: bean validation fires on **update** as well as insert, so annotating a column that already holds blank Arabic makes every such existing record **uneditable** until someone supplies the Arabic |
| **Prerequisite discovered (and the reason this is not a one-line fix)** | The write paths that most need the constraint bind the JPA entity straight from the request body with no DTO and no `@Valid` — `create(@RequestBody ChangeRequest body)`, the F2-22a class — so the **entity** constraint is the only available lever. And an entity constraint fails at **flush**, inside the transaction, so Spring wraps it in `TransactionSystemException` at commit. **Nothing handled that**, so every entity-level constraint in the platform would have surfaced as a bare **500**. The pre-existing `handleConstraint` looked like it covered this — its javadoc claimed it stopped "Hibernate's constraint failures" reaching 500 — but it only catches a violation that propagates *unwrapped*, i.e. the `@Validated` service-method case that never touches a transaction. Adding `@NotBlank` without closing that leg would have converted *"you forgot the Arabic title"* into an opaque server error: **the platform would have become worse at explaining a failure it had just started detecting** |
| **Recommendation** | 1. **Close the handler leg first** — map `TransactionSystemException`, unwrap the violation, emit the same `errors` map shape as the MVC handler. 2. **Enforce in tranches, gated on measured blank-row counts**, never wholesale. 3. **`@Valid` on the entity-bound write paths** in each tranche so the 400 comes from the well-trodden MVC handler. 4. **Annotate the DTO too** where a real DTO exists — that is the actual write path; the entity constraint is the backstop for service-layer saves. 5. **A ratchet test** with the data-blocked pairs enumerated *with their row counts*, so the debt is quantified rather than excused |
| **Alternatives** | (a) `@Column(nullable = false)` instead of `@NotBlank` — rejected: Hibernate fails it as `PropertyValueException` → **500**, and it does not catch the empty string, which is most of what is actually in these columns. (b) A DB `NOT NULL` migration — rejected: needs a backfill, and inventing Arabic governance text is exactly the fabrication this program refuses (same reasoning as V318 linking nobody and V319 flipping nothing). (c) Enforce all 157 at once — rejected on measurement: it strands 293 live records. (d) Validate in the frontend only — rejected: the API is the contract, and integrations bypass the UI |
| **Trade-offs** | Enforcing on a populated column trades "records can be English-only" for "records with no Arabic cannot be edited until fixed". That is the right trade **only** where the count is zero, which is why the count gates the tranche. The 35 data-blocked pairs are left deliberately unenforced: a product decision (who supplies the Arabic for 293 existing rows?) masquerading as an engineering one would be the wrong call to make unilaterally |
| **Effort** | **S** for the mechanism + first tranche + ratchet (~3 h actual). **M** for the remaining 122 clean pairs, mechanical but wide. The 35 data-blocked pairs are not engineering-bound at all |
| **Priority** | **Medium.** It has no exploit, but it is the cheapest defence of the platform's central product claim, and every day it stays unenforced adds rows that make the eventual fix harder |
| **Expected ROI** | High for the ratchet specifically: it converts an aspirational house rule into a measured number that can only go down, and it prices the remaining work exactly (157 pairs, 35 blocked, 293 rows) |
| **Verification** | **Suite 2,525 / 0 / 0** (+3 bilingual ratchet, +5 handler tests, −5 net from consolidating the constraint handlers). Ratchet reports **157 asymmetric of 574 pairs**, baseline 157, decreasing-only. **Live 6/6 through the Vite proxy:** change create without `titleAr` → **400 with `errors: {titleAr}`** (was silently accepted); with Arabic → 201; update keeping Arabic → 200 (nothing broke); update *stripping* Arabic → 400 naming the field; committee create without `nameAr` → 400 naming it (the DTO leg); and **`Risk` create English-only → still 201**, proving the data-blocked set is genuinely untouched rather than accidentally enforced. Browser-verified the response the FE actually receives: `{status: 400, title: "Validation Failed", errors: {titleAr: "Field must not be blank"}}`. The wrapped-at-commit leg is pinned by unit test rather than probe because only one of the two throw sites is reachable through a controller — including the negative: a commit failure with **no** constraint violation stays a 500 rather than being dressed up as a client error |
| **Status** | 🟡 **Tranche 1 CLOSED 2026-07-26.** Mechanism fixed; 8 fields annotated across 7 entities (`ChangeRequest.titleAr`, `ChangeTask.titleAr`, `ChangeFreezeWindow.nameAr`, `Committee.nameAr`, `CommitteeResolution.titleAr` + `bodyAr`, `CommitteeActionItem.titleAr`, `CommitteeMeetingPaper.titleAr`) — the change and committee governance backbone, so **a board resolution can no longer be recorded in English only**. 122 clean pairs remain enforceable; 35 are data-blocked. Ratchet baseline 157 |
| **References** | `common/exception/GlobalExceptionHandler.java` (`handleTransactionSystem` + shared `constraintProblem`), `BilingualInvariantRatchetTest`, `EntityValidationHandlerTest`, `CommitteeDtos.CommitteeCreateRequest`. Carried in: **F2-25** deferred `memberNameAr` here, and it stays deferred — `admin.committee_member_terms` has no `*_ar` column at all, so it is an `EN_ONLY` case needing a migration. Related: **F2-22a** (the entity-bound `@RequestBody` sites that forced the entity-level lever) |
| **Lesson** | The measurement was wrong the first time in the same direction as F2-27's, on the same day: a source-level regex found **103** asymmetric pairs and 375 total, reflection found **157** and 574. Twice now, a grep has under-reported a defect and the smaller number read as reassurance. **Reflection reads the annotations the JVM enforces; a regex reads what a human wrote near them.** The second lesson is about enforcement order: the instinct was to add the constraint, and the constraint would have produced a 500 — before enforcing an invariant, check what the failure will actually look like to the user, because a detected error reported badly can be worse than an undetected one |

## F2-42 · There are two board packs — the real one on the server, and a fabricated one on screen

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-42 |
| **Category** | Architecture / façade island / fabricated executive reporting |
| **Severity** | **P1 High** — the board pack is the single artefact most likely to be cited verbatim in a board or audit-committee meeting, and the on-screen version contains no tenant data at all |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-26 while closing **W2-1a-ii**, whose scope was "de-mock `lib/boardPack.ts` (3 mock imports)". Measuring the file showed the three `mock*` imports were the minority of its fabricated inputs, and that a real server-side board pack already exists |
| **Current State** | **`GET /api/v1/reporting/board-pack.pdf` and `.pptx` are real.** `BoardPackService` and `BoardPackPptxService` read live repositories — `OrganisationRepository`, `RiskRepository`, `AuditFindingRepository`, `KriDefinitionRepository`, `KriMeasurementRepository`, `ChangeRequestRepository`, `EthicsCaseRepository`, `AmlAlertRepository` — and F2-27's probes returned a 40 KB PDF and a 34 KB PPTX from the running stack. The dashboard already links to both. **Meanwhile the `/board-pack` route renders something else entirely:** `BoardPackPage` calls `buildBoardPack()` from `lib/boardPack.ts`, which assembles its payload from **eight hardcoded literal arrays** — `SAUDI_APPETITES`, `APPROVAL_REQUESTS`, `CCM_CHECKS`, `ASSETS`, `CHAIN_RULES`, `ALL_WORKFLOWS` (`mockWorkflows.ts`), `AUDIT_ENGAGEMENTS` (`mockCompliance`) and `BREACHES` (`mockPrivacy`) — across 13 usage sites. Its own header comment reads *"Consolidates live signal from every module"* |
| **Problem** | An executive opening the board pack **page** sees appetite breaches, board-level approvals, audit milestones, PDPL 72-hour breach posture, CCM coverage, asset blast-radius and SLA figures that belong to no tenant. They are not stale, not partial, and not approximate — they are invented, and they are formatted to be quoted. Worse, the page and the export disagree: the same platform will hand a board two different sets of numbers for the same period depending on whether the user reads the screen or downloads the PDF, and the PDF is the correct one |
| **Root Cause** | The frontend aggregator was written in Sprint 5 to demonstrate the board pack before the backend service existed; the backend one arrived later and nothing retired the frontend duplicate. This is the **same shape as PM-C1**, the policy façade that sat beside the real policy backend until CH-6/CH-9 consolidated it — and the same shape as the F2-15 mock-file debt generally. What makes it P1 rather than P2 is which artefact it is |
| **Business Impact** | Board and audit-committee reporting is the platform's highest-trust output and a primary reason a Saudi listed entity buys it. A demo that opens `/board-pack` shows a plausible, fully-populated executive pack for an empty tenant — impressive until someone checks a number. In production it means a board could minute a figure the platform invented |
| **Technical Impact** | The fix is a **consolidation decision, not a rewire**, and that is the finding's most useful conclusion: wiring eight fabricated arrays to live APIs would be a day of work reconstructing on the client what `BoardPackService` already computes correctly on the server. Two viable shapes: **(a)** add a JSON sibling to the existing PDF/PPTX endpoints and have the page render that, or **(b)** retire the page and route "Board pack" to the real export, which the dashboard already links |
| **UX Impact** | Option (a) keeps an on-screen pack and makes it true. Option (b) removes a browsable view in exchange for a downloaded file. Either is better than the present state, where the screen and the file disagree and only the file is right |
| **Recommendation** | 1. **Do not rewire `lib/boardPack.ts`.** Confirm which of (a)/(b) is wanted — it changes a user-visible route, so it is a product call. 2. Recommended: **(a)**, exposing `BoardPackService`'s aggregate as JSON under `REPORTING:EXPORT`'s sibling `REPORTING:READ`, because a board pack you can only download is a worse product than one you can also read. 3. Delete `lib/boardPack.ts` and `mockWorkflows.ts` with it (F2-15 counts them among the 15 mock files / 3,650 lines to remove). 4. Until then the page should carry a visible "sample data" banner — an honest label is cheap and the current silence is the actual defect |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Wire the eight arrays to live APIs — rejected: duplicates server logic on the client, and the two implementations would drift immediately. (b) Leave it and rely on the export — rejected: the page is reachable, linked and looks authoritative. (c) Delete the page with no replacement — viable but loses a real capability; the export is not a substitute for a readable view |
| **Trade-offs** | Consolidating means the on-screen pack gains a server round-trip and loses the ability to render with the backend down. That is the right trade for an artefact whose whole value is that its numbers are correct |
| **Effort** | **S** for the banner and the decision. **M** for option (a) — one JSON endpoint reusing the existing service, one page rewrite, deleting two mock files |
| **Priority** | **High.** It is cheap relative to its blast radius, and it is exactly the class of defect the independent assessment was commissioned to find |
| **Verification** | Measured 2026-07-26: `BoardPackService` + `BoardPackPptxService` field lists (8 live repositories); F2-27's live probes (`board-pack.pdf` 40 397 bytes, `board-pack.pptx` 34 905 bytes, both 200 for `viewer` and `admin`); `BoardPackPage.tsx:30` `useMemo(() => buildBoardPack(), [])`; `lib/boardPack.ts` 8 hardcoded array imports across 13 usage sites |
| **Status** | ⬜ **OPEN — blocked on a product decision** (a) or (b). Deliberately NOT half-fixed in W2-1a-ii; a partial rewire would have left the page fabricated while looking addressed |
| **References** | `lib/boardPack.ts`, `pages/board-pack/BoardPackPage.tsx:30`, `module/reporting/service/BoardPackService.java:67-74`, `BoardPackPptxService.java:60-67`, `ExportController` board-pack endpoints. Same class as **PM-C1** (policy façade, since consolidated) and **F2-15** (mock-file debt). Found by **W2-1a-ii** |
| **Lesson** | The plan sized this as "3 mock imports in one file". The file had eight fabricated inputs and a correct server-side twin — so the scoped task was not just too small, it pointed the wrong way: the work is *deletion and consolidation*, not migration. Third time in two days that reading the code changed the shape of the task rather than only its size (F2-27 24→40 endpoints, F2-30 103→157 pairs, this). **Read the thing before believing a count of it** — and when a fabricated frontend surface exists, check whether the backend already does it properly before wiring anything |

---

## F2-43 · Scheduled reporting was unreachable — `REPORTING:WRITE` was held by zero of 57 roles

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-43 |
| **Category** | Authorisation / dead grant / unreachable feature — the **F2-23 class** |
| **Severity** | **P2 Medium** — a whole shipped feature was inaccessible to every persona, but fail-closed: no data was exposed and no wrong answer was produced |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-26 by **F2-13a**, the first run of `Appendix/scripts/grant_tightness.py`, which joins the compiled `@PreAuthorize` set to `iam.role_permissions`. Not findable from either side alone — the guard looks ordinary and the catalogue row exists |
| **Current State** | `REPORTING:WRITE` guarded `ReportScheduleController#create`, `#update` and `#runNow` — the entire write surface of scheduled reporting — and **0 of 57 roles held it**. The guards are permission-only, with no ORed `hasAnyRole` leg to fall through to, so every request 403'd except `PLATFORM_ADMIN`'s, which passes on `ModulePermissionEvaluator`'s hardcoded code bypass rather than on any grant. Meanwhile **18 roles held `REPORTING:READ`** and could list schedules they could never create, and `ORG_ADMIN` held `REPORTING:DELETE` — able to delete a schedule it could neither create nor list |
| **Problem** | `ReportSchedulerService` ticks every minute, regenerates a PDF/XLSX via `ExportService` and emails it — the platform's only unattended reporting path, and the answer to "can this send the board its pack automatically". A tenant could see the schedule list, see the empty state, and have no way to add a row. The failure mode is silence: 403 is not logged as an error, and whoever tested the feature almost certainly tested as `admin`, for whom it worked |
| **Root Cause** | The permission was catalogued and the guards written; the grant rows were never authored. Identical to **F2-23** (`ADMIN:APPROVE` guarding 59 committee methods while absent from the catalogue) and **F2-27** (`AI:EXPORT` absent while 41 roles held `AI:READ`) — **the sixth instance of one shape: the mechanism was correct and its data or precondition never existed.** What made this one invisible for longer is that `PLATFORM_ADMIN`'s bypass is indistinguishable from a working grant |
| **Business Impact** | Unattended regulatory and board reporting is a buying reason, not a nicety — SAMA/NCA reporting cadence is exactly what a scheduled export is for. A demo run as `admin` shows the feature working; the first customer administrator to try it cannot create a schedule and gets no explanation |
| **Technical Impact** | Repaired by **V321**: `REPORTING:WRITE` + `REPORTING:READ` to `ORG_ADMIN` (already trusted with `REPORTING:DELETE`) and `GRC_ADMINISTRATOR` (the tenant's GRC operations admin, which held no `REPORTING` permission at all). Deliberately **not** granted to the 18 `REPORTING:READ` holders: creating a schedule mails a whole register to an arbitrary address on a cron, so create is an administrative act. Deliberately **not** granted `REPORTING:EXPORT` — F2-27 made that a live file-download right and widening exfiltration is not part of unblocking a feature |
| **UX Impact** | Before: the create form 403'd with a bare "Access Denied". After: two admin personas can schedule, and the read-only 18 still cannot — which is the correct shape, not a compromise |
| **Recommendation** | 1. **V321** as shipped. 2. Keep `grant_tightness.py` in the release checklist — this class is undetectable by inspection and trivial to re-introduce, because adding a `hasPermission` guard needs no corresponding grant to compile, boot, or pass tests. 3. When a permission-only guard is added, the same commit should either grant it or state why nobody holds it |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Grant to all 18 `REPORTING:READ` holders — rejected: read access to a register is not authority to email it on a schedule. (b) Change the guards to `REPORTING:READ` — rejected: it would make scheduling available to `VIEWER` and `REGULATOR_VIEWER`, which is the F2-27 defect in reverse. (c) Leave it platform-admin-only — rejected: a tenant that cannot administer its own scheduled reports does not own the feature |
| **Trade-offs** | Two roles is deliberately tight, and some tenants will want `COMPLIANCE_OFFICER` scheduling reports. That is a role-editor change, not a code change — the reversible direction. The opposite mistake (granting broadly now) is the one F2-13b exists to undo |
| **Effort** | **XS** — one idempotent migration, four grant rows |
| **Priority** | **Done.** Cheap, and it closed the only section-A2 instance the report found |
| **Verification** | V321 applied twice to dev via pyodbc, second pass delta 0 (`REPORTING:WRITE` holders 0→2, `READ` 18→20, `role_permissions` 1435→1439). Live 13/13 through the running stack: `viewer` GET 200 / POST 403 / PUT 403 / run-now 403 (holds `READ`+`EXPORT`, never `WRITE`); `compliance` GET 200 / POST **201** / PUT 200 / run-now 200 with a `SUCCESS` `ReportRun`; `compliance` DELETE still **403** (`REPORTING:DELETE` not granted). `organisationId` stamped 1 from `TenantContext`, not from the body. Test rows removed with `CHARINDEX`. Re-running the report shows section A2 = 0 |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-26** — V321 + `grant_tightness.py`. Exposed **F2-44** on the way through |
| **References** | `V321__reporting_write_grant.sql`, `module/reporting/controller/ReportScheduleController.java:38,43,54`, `ReportSchedulerService`, `Appendix/scripts/grant_tightness.py` §A2, `Appendix/GRANT_TIGHTNESS.md`, `PermissionCatalogueUsageTest`. Same class as **F2-23**, **F2-27** |
| **Lesson** | The plan's own illustration of this class was "`ADMIN:WRITE` is in 0 roles". Measurement says `ADMIN:WRITE` is held by **3** — the plan's example had already been fixed, and the real instance was a permission the plan never named. **A finding's illustrative example decays like any other measurement**; re-measure before quoting it. And the reason this needed a script rather than a careful reading: the two facts live in a Java annotation and a SQL table that no build step, test, or code review ever compares |

---

## F2-44 · The reporting service accepted Arabic names and threw them away

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-44 |
| **Category** | Bilingual invariant / silent field drop — the **F2-30 class**, on the write path |
| **Severity** | **P3 Low** — one entity, and the surface was unreachable until the same day it was found |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-26 by the **F2-43** live probe, at probe 8 of 13. The probe asserted the Arabic name round-tripped because CLAUDE.md's bilingual rule says it must; it came back missing. Not visible in any code review of the diff, because the omission is in a file the diff did not touch |
| **Current State** | `ReportScheduleService.apply()` copies caller-settable fields one by one. `nameAr` and `descriptionAr` — the two columns V20 added to `reporting.report_schedules` — were **not in the list**. The endpoint accepted them, Jackson bound them, the entity mapped them, and the service dropped both without an error. `POST` returned **201** with the Arabic name silently absent |
| **Problem** | Arabic is the platform's primary language for a Saudi tenant. A bilingual administrator names a scheduled report in Arabic, gets a 201, and the Arabic name is gone — with no message, no validation error, and a row that looks saved. 8 of the 11 seeded schedules *do* carry Arabic names, because the seed script writes the table directly; the API never could |
| **Root Cause** | A hand-written field-by-field copy loop, added before V20 introduced the Arabic columns and never revisited. The class of defect is structural: every such loop silently omits whatever is added to the entity afterwards. **It survived because the endpoints were unreachable** — no role held `REPORTING:WRITE`, so in the platform's entire history not one create had ever completed. F2-43 and F2-44 are one causal chain: the dead grant hid the dropped field |
| **Business Impact** | Small in isolation, but it is the platform's core bilingual promise failing on a write path, and it fails in the most expensive way — invisibly, on a record the user believes they saved |
| **Technical Impact** | Two lines in `apply()`. `ReportScheduleBilingualTest` (3 tests) pins create, update, and — the case a naive fix breaks — that an **omitted** Arabic field is left alone rather than blanked, so an English-only rename cannot wipe an existing Arabic name |
| **UX Impact** | An Arabic-named schedule now persists and renders. No visible change for English-only tenants |
| **Recommendation** | 1. Fixed as shipped. 2. **F2-30 tranche 2 should sweep hand-written copy loops, not only entity annotations** — `@NotBlank` on `nameAr` would have turned this silent drop into a 400 only if the field reached the entity, which it never did. A `*_ar` column that no service copies is invisible to a validation-based ratchet. 3. Candidate sweep: services with an `apply(entity, entity)` shape and a bilingual entity |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Replace the copy loop with a bean-mapper — rejected here: the explicit loop is what keeps `id`, `organisationId`, `externalId`, the audit stamps and the `lastRun*` outcome fields server-owned on an endpoint that binds the entity straight from the request body (F2-22's shape). A mapper would have to re-express all of that as exclusions. (b) Add `@NotBlank nameAr` — deferred: 4 of 11 existing rows are blank, so it would make them uneditable; the F2-30 data-blocked rule applies |
| **Trade-offs** | Keeping the explicit loop keeps this defect possible for the next column added. The test is the mitigation, and it is cheaper than the alternative's exclusion list |
| **Effort** | **XS** — two lines, three tests |
| **Priority** | **Done** — found and fixed inside the same slice |
| **Verification** | Live: probe 8 failed (`nameAr` absent) before the fix, passed after a recompile and DevTools restart, 13/13. `ReportScheduleBilingualTest` 3/3. Full suite **2,538/0/0**. Measured: `reporting.report_schedules` 11 rows, 4 with blank `name_ar` |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-26** |
| **References** | `module/reporting/service/ReportScheduleService.java` `apply()`, `ReportScheduleBilingualTest`, `ReportSchedule` entity (V20 added `name_ar`/`description_ar`). Same invariant as **F2-30**; exposed by **F2-43** |
| **Lesson** | **Unblocking a feature is where its untested code paths surface.** The slice's scope was a report and a grant migration; the moment a request could actually complete, the first thing behind the guard was broken. Two corollaries: a live probe that asserts the *house rules* rather than only the *change* is what caught it — the bilingual assertion had nothing to do with authorisation — and a defect can stay invisible not because it is subtle but because the code path was closed |

## F2-40 · 20 board resolutions carried a tally the voting engine would have silently overwritten

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-40 |
| **Category** | Governance integrity / provenance — **F2-28's class, on the resolution instead of the meeting** |
| **Severity** | **P1 High** — one vote on any of 20 APPROVED board resolutions would have rewritten its recorded outcome, and the referral gate that authorises changes and policies reads `outcome` and `voteFor` straight off the resolution |
| **Provenance** | Carried out of **F2-28** (round-2 review, third addition) as "20 resolutions whose tallies are seeded scalars with zero vote rows"; scoped as **S** and deferred to sit with F2-29. Measured and fixed 2026-07-26 while ranking F2-22b's 214 entity-bound writes — `CommitteeResolutionController#create` ranked CRIT on `voteFor`/`voteAgainst`/`voteAbstain`/`outcome`, which is what pointed here |
| **Current State** | `admin.committee_resolutions` held **20 rows**; `admin.committee_resolution_vote` held **zero**. All 20 carried a scalar tally and outcome `APPROVED` (5/0/0, 4/1/0, 4/0/1 …). `recomputeTally()` re-derives `vote_for`/`against`/`abstain` from the vote rows and maps them onto an outcome — with no vote rows that is `0/0/0 → DEFERRED` — and its only caller is `recordVote()`. So **one member casting one vote on any of those resolutions would have rewritten a 5/0/0 APPROVED board decision to 1/0/0**, and a `RECUSED` or `ABSTAIN` vote would have produced `0/0/0 DEFERRED`. Nothing warned; the audit line would have reported the new tally as the true one |
| **Problem** | A board resolution is the platform's highest-authority record. Silently converting an approved one to deferred is worse than losing it, because everything downstream keeps working: Track D asserts `CAB_APPROVED` on quorate approved minutes, and `CommitteeReferralService` reads the resolution's outcome to authorise a change or publish a policy. The defect needed no attacker — a member using the voting panel the platform ships would have triggered it |
| **Root Cause** | Two legitimate ways to establish a tally with nothing distinguishing them. A committee that votes in the room has its decision typed in by the secretariat (`CommitteesPage`'s resolution form posts the three counts and the outcome); a committee that votes in-platform has it derived from vote rows. One column would have separated them and there was none — exactly F2-28's shape, where `quorumMet` from `complete` was indistinguishable from a computed quorum |
| **Business Impact** | Board and committee minutes are the artefact a regulator, external auditor or court reads. A platform that can quietly restate a carried motion as deferred cannot be the system of record for Saudi board governance, and the loss would surface months later with no trace of what it had been |
| **Technical Impact** | **V322** adds `tally_source` (`COMPUTED` \| `ASSERTED` \| `ASSERTED_LEGACY`) with a CHECK, plus `tally_recorded_by_user_id`, `tally_recorded_by_name`, `tally_stamped_at`. `create()` stamps `ASSERTED` + the authenticated recorder when the body carries counts, `COMPUTED` when it does not; `recomputeTally()` claims `COMPUTED` and clears the asserter; `recordVote()` **refuses with 409** on an asserted resolution rather than converting it. The four provenance fields are `@JsonProperty(READ_ONLY)` — a caller cannot post their own asserter, which is the DF-3 rule applied to the tally |
| **UX Impact** | The resolution register now shows a `from minutes` / `legacy` badge beside the tally, bilingual, with the recorder in the tooltip. A reader can tell a counted tally from a recorded one — which they could not before, and which is the whole finding |
| **Recommendation** | 1. V322 + the service changes as shipped. 2. The 20 legacy rows keep their numbers and their `APPROVED` outcome, labelled `ASSERTED_LEGACY` with **no invented attribution**. 3. If a committee needs to move a resolution from asserted to voted, that is a new explicit action, not a side effect of casting a vote. 4. The same question should be asked of every other scalar that can arrive either derived or asserted — `complianceScore`, `riskScore`, `maturityLevel` all appear in the F2-22b CRIT list |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **Refuse the caller's tally** — the obvious fix, and wrong: it would silently discard the recorded decision the secretariat form exists to capture. Same trap as F2-22a's prescribed `@JsonIgnore`, which would have stripped `id` from every response. (b) Backfill vote rows from the scalars — rejected: it would fabricate named votes nobody cast, the exact defect DF-3 closed. (c) Let `recomputeTally` convert asserted to computed — rejected: there is no correct arithmetic, because the asserted counts include members who have no vote row and never will. (d) Flip the 20 legacy rows to `DEFERRED` for consistency — rejected on the same grounds as V315/V316/V319: a new control must not retroactively invalidate approved governance history to make itself look clean |
| **Trade-offs** | An asserted resolution can no longer be voted on in-platform, so a committee that recorded a paper tally and then wanted to re-run the motion electronically must create a new resolution. That is the correct direction to be inflexible in |
| **Effort** | **S** as scoped, and it held: one migration, one entity, three service methods, one badge |
| **Priority** | **Done** |
| **Verification** | V322 applied twice to dev, second pass byte-identical; 20/20 rows labelled `ASSERTED_LEGACY`, `APPROVED` count 20 and `SUM(vote_for)` 85 **unchanged** by the migration, no attribution invented. Live **17/17**: a tally from the minutes returns 5/0/0 APPROVED stamped `ASSERTED`/`compliance`; a body forging `tallySource: COMPUTED` + `tallyRecordedByName: the_chairman` + a 2001 timestamp is ignored on all three fields; an empty resolution is `COMPUTED` and a real vote yields 1/0/0 APPROVED COMPUTED; a vote on the asserted resolution **409s** naming `5/0/0` and its recorder, with the row untouched in the database; a vote on legacy `RES-2026-Q1-BRD-001` 409s admitting the provenance is unrecoverable; **no vote row was written by either refused call**. `ResolutionTallyProvenanceTest` 6/6, committee suite 185/0 across 10 classes, browser-verified: the badge renders on all 20 rows in Arabic with the tooltip, clean console. Test rows removed with `CHARINDEX`; register back to 20 rows / 85 votes |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-26** — V322 |
| **References** | `V322__resolution_tally_provenance.sql`, `CommitteeResolutionService` (`create`, `recomputeTally`, `recordVote`), `CommitteeResolution` entity, `CommitteesPage.tsx` resolution register, `ResolutionTallyProvenanceTest`. Carried from **F2-28**; same disposition as **V315**/**V316**/**V319**; surfaced by **F2-22b**'s risk ranking |
| **Lesson** | **The second time in one day that the prescribed fix was the wrong one, and the UI is what falsified it both times.** F2-13a's plan row cited a stale example; here the plan's own phrasing ("seeded scalar tallies … caller-authored") invited "refuse the caller's tally", which would have broken the minute-taking form a board actually uses. The distinction that matters is not *who supplied a value* but *whether the system can tell how it was established* — and where two legitimate sources exist, the fix is a provenance column, never a prohibition. Also: the guard's first version keyed on the label alone and instantly broke 22 existing provenance tests, because an entity constructed outside `create()` has no label. Keying it on *what would actually be overwritten* fixed them for the right reason — **when a new guard breaks old tests, check whether the guard is too broad before changing the tests** |

## F2-22b(i) · The cross-tenant write backstop watched updates and ignored inserts

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-22b(i) — first tranche of F2-22b |
| **Category** | Multi-tenancy / defence-in-depth gap — a control that covered the wrong half |
| **Severity** | **P2 Medium** as measured, **P1 if any create endpoint ever misses its tenant stamp.** No live cross-tenant write exists today: all 202 endpoints whose body type exposes `organisationId` sit behind a service that stamps it from `TenantContext`. The finding is that the mechanism designed to catch the *first miss* could not see it |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-26 by following F2-22b's own risk ranking. `organisationId` is bindable on **148 of the 214** entity-bound writes, so the question was whether that is exploitable; answering it meant reading the backstop that is supposed to make it moot, and the backstop had one `@Override` |
| **Current State** | `TenantWriteGuardInterceptor` implemented **only `onFlushDirty`** — Hibernate's *update* hook. `decide()` compares `previousState[orgIdx]` to `currentState[orgIdx]`, so it fires only when an existing row's tenant *changes*. An **INSERT** has no previous state and `onSave` was never implemented, so a create carrying another tenant's `organisationId` was never examined. Meanwhile `application-prod.yml` turns the bean ON and describes it as the "platform-wide Hibernate-level cross-tenant write backstop … so a single missed per-service guard cannot silently write across tenants" — true for updates, false for creates. Compounding it: the flag is set **only** in prod, so the control ran exclusively where nobody could observe it, and `SecurityException` had **no handler**, so a block surfaced as **500** despite the class javadoc claiming "response is 403" |
| **Problem** | The uncovered half is the likelier one. An update inherits its tenant from the row it loaded; a *create* has no row to inherit from, so a new endpoint that forgets `setOrganisationId(TenantContext.getCurrentTenant())` takes the tenant from the request body — and that is precisely the omission the backstop exists to survive. The three defects this platform found by hand (DF-3, DF-4, F2-25) were all on write paths, and F2-25's was a create |
| **Root Cause** | The interceptor was written against the "id-bearing body → `merge()` → cross-tenant overwrite" attack, which is an update. Inserts were not part of that threat model and nothing revisited it. The dev/prod flag split then removed every chance of noticing: in dev the bean is inert, in prod the half that ran had no hole |
| **Business Impact** | Multi-tenant isolation is the platform's foundational security claim for a shared Saudi deployment, and this is the control an auditor would be shown as evidence of defence in depth. "Covers updates only" is not what its config comment says it does |
| **Technical Impact** | `onSave` implemented, delegating to a new package-private `decideInsert()`. The rule differs from the update rule — an insert has no previous state, so the invariant is *the new row must belong to the request's tenant*. Two deliberate exemptions: a **null** `organisationId` (a NOT NULL violation about to happen, which the database reports far better) and **no tenant on the thread** (schedulers and sweeps run off-request, where `TenantContext` is unset; throwing there would break every sweep, in production only). `SecurityException` now maps to **403** with a `cross-tenant-write` ProblemDetail whose body deliberately does not echo the org ids. **The flag is now ON in dev**, because a security control that runs only in production is tested only by customers |
| **UX Impact** | A refused write returns 403 with a clear message instead of a generic 500, so the client can distinguish a refusal from a bug — and so can whoever watches the 500 rate |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. Every legitimate cross-tenant write must opt in via `TenantContext.allowCrossTenantWrite()`; `LicensingService.upsert` now does. 3. F2-22b's remaining tranches should keep using the risk ranking, and should keep in mind that a *bindable* field is not an *exploitable* one — 202 of 202 are defended per-service today |
| **Alternatives** | (a) A global Jackson rule making `organisationId` non-deserialisable on every `@Entity` — considered and rejected for this tranche: `LicensingService` legitimately requires it from its argument, so the rule needs a per-type allowlist, and it would silently change 148 endpoints' binding behaviour in one commit. The interceptor is the narrower, louder change. (b) Add the missing stamps per-service — there are none missing; that is what the measurement showed. (c) Leave the insert leg out and rely on the per-service stamps — rejected: that is exactly the "one missed guard" the backstop exists for |
| **Trade-offs** | Enabling the guard in dev risks tripping a legitimate flow that prod has already soaked. That is the point — dev is where a trip should be discovered. Probes 4–9 confirm ordinary creates, updates and reads are unaffected |
| **Effort** | **S** — one interceptor method, one exception handler, one config line, one opt-in scope |
| **Priority** | **Done** |
| **Verification** | **Mutation-proven end to end.** With the controller's tenant stamp and the service's opt-in scope both removed, granting org 2 a module it does not hold returned **403** with `type: …/errors/cross-tenant-write` and **wrote nothing**; both restored, the same call is a normal 200. That is also the proof the flag is live in dev, since the interceptor is the only thing that could have refused it. `TenantWriteGuardInterceptorTest` **15/15** (7 new: blocks a foreign-tenant insert, allows own-tenant, allows inside the opt-in scope and blocks again after it closes, allows when no tenant is on the thread, allows a null tenant on the row, ignores entities with no tenant column, and never reports having modified Hibernate's state array). Live **12/12**: hostile `organisationId` in a change create still lands in org 1; ordinary creates/updates/reads unaffected with the guard on; no probe row outside org 1; licence table verified back to its seed counts. Hibernate 6.2.13's `Interceptor` bytecode confirms the `Object`-id `onSave` overload is the dispatch entry point |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-26.** F2-22b itself stays open — DTO conversion of the 54 CRIT endpoints has not started |
| **References** | `TenantWriteGuardInterceptor` (`onSave`, `decideInsert`), `TenantWriteGuardConfig`, `application-dev.yml`, `GlobalExceptionHandler#handleTenantIsolation`, `LicensingService#upsert`, `TenantWriteGuardInterceptorTest`. Ranking from `MassAssignmentRatchetTest#riskProfileIsRecordedForF2_22b` → `target/mass-assignment-risk.txt` |
| **Lesson** | **A control that only runs in production is only tested by customers.** The insert gap was invisible for its whole life because the flag was prod-only — and the same split hid that a block returned 500 rather than the 403 its own javadoc promised. Two process notes worth more than the fix: the first mutation probe reported the guard as dead when it had simply targeted a licence row that **already existed**, making the call an UPDATE that never reached `onSave` — *when a security probe says "not blocked", check that the probe exercised the path before believing it*. And this probe wrote through seeded rows and then deleted one it had not created, needing three separate repairs; a probe must **capture and restore what it alters and delete only what it inserted** |

---

## F2-22b(ii) · A BCM record could be created already approved, by anyone who could create one

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-22b(ii) — second tranche of F2-22b |
| **Category** | Authorisation / state-machine bypass + forged approval provenance — **DF-4's shape on new surfaces** |
| **Severity** | **P1 High.** Not a theoretical binding: `POST /api/v1/bcm/cycles {"status":"APPROVED","approvedBy":"the_ceo"}` created an approved BCM assessment cycle attributed to a name the caller chose, and because `updateCycle` refuses to edit an APPROVED cycle the forged record was then immutable except by transition. Live-reproduced before the fix |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-26 by working the CRIT tier of F2-22b's risk ranking in order. 26 of the 54 CRIT endpoints were CRIT because they exposed `approvedBy`/`approvedAt`; reading the BCM cluster's services showed the status field was the sharper half |
| **Current State** | Three creates read `if (status == null) status = "DRAFT"` and then validated the value against a vocabulary **containing `APPROVED`** — `BcmCycleService.createCycle`, `createGovernance` (whose set also contains `ACTIVE`), and `BcmBiaAssessmentService.create`. All approval on these entities is owned by a `/transition` endpoint that enforces the chain (DRAFT → IN_PROGRESS → UNDER_REVIEW → APPROVED), stamps `approvedBy` from the authenticated actor, writes a TRANSITION audit row, and — for a BIA — gates on `mtpdHours` being set and `RTO ≤ MTPD`. A create supplying the end state skipped every one of those. Separately, `approvedBy`/`approvedAt` were deserialisable on **21** entity-bound types across BCM, NDI, sustainability, privacy, policy, cyber and TPRM |
| **Problem** | For a BIA the bypass is worse than a forged signature: `APPROVED` requires `mtpdHours` and `RTO ≤ MTPD`, so a create could produce an approved Business Impact Analysis with **no maximum tolerable period of disruption and an unchecked recovery objective** — the number every downstream BCP, DR plan and NCEMA submission is derived from. For a governance record it publishes an approved BCM policy without the review chain |
| **Root Cause** | Two independent halves that only bite together: a create that treats the full status vocabulary as valid input, and provenance fields the caller can set. The platform had already fixed exactly this twice — DF-3 (the vote named its own voter), DF-4 (a requester approved their own change by submitting another name) — and F2-40 a few hours earlier on a resolution's tally. Nothing generalised the rule |
| **Business Impact** | BCM approval evidence is what a NCEMA or SAMA reviewer asks for by name. A record that says "approved by the CEO" when no approval happened is worse than a missing one, and the state machine that was supposed to prevent it works correctly — it was simply not the only way in |
| **Technical Impact** | `approvedBy`/`approvedAt` are now `@JsonProperty(READ_ONLY)` on all **21** entities (still serialised — the F2-22a inverse check is asserted, because hiding the approver from responses would break every screen and export that shows it). The three creates now **reject** a non-initial state with a message naming the field and the transition endpoint, rather than silently forcing DRAFT — silently dropping a field the caller sent is F2-44, closed the same day. The risk ranking now skips fields already `READ_ONLY`, so it measures progress instead of being a static census: **CRIT 54 → 28** |
| **UX Impact** | None for honest callers. A UI that posts `status: 'DRAFT'` explicitly still works — asserted, because that is what `NdiPage` does |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. **The remaining CRIT 28 are mostly `status`/`outcome`/score fields** — each needs the same treatment: read the state machine that owns it, then reject rather than ignore. 3. Three DTO-based services accept a caller-supplied approver — `BoardPackService` sets `approvedBy` from the request in its APPROVED branch, plus `CptyService` and `TreatmentPlanService`. **They are invisible to this ranking because they take DTOs, which is the useful correction: converting an endpoint to a DTO does not fix mass assignment if the DTO carries the same field.** |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Silently force the initial state — rejected, that is F2-44's silent-drop defect. (b) Remove `APPROVED` from the create-time vocabulary only — same effect, but the vocabulary is shared with the transition validator and splitting it invites drift. (c) Full DTOs for the three creates — larger change, and per the note above it would not have fixed the provenance half by itself |
| **Trade-offs** | A caller who *was* relying on create-with-status now gets a 400. No shipped UI does; an integration might, and the message tells it exactly what to call instead |
| **Effort** | **S** for this tranche. F2-22b overall stays **L** |
| **Priority** | **Done** |
| **Verification** | Live **18/18**: create with `status=APPROVED` → **400** naming `status=APPROVED` and `/cycles/{id}/transition`, with **nothing written**; mid-chain `UNDER_REVIEW` also 400; the honest create lands DRAFT with the forged `approvedBy`/`approvedAt` **not bound**; the real chain still runs and stamps `approvedBy=admin` — *the authenticated actor, not the `the_ceo` in the body* — with a server-side timestamp; a BIA create with `approvalStatus=APPROVED` is refused with the MTPD gate named; `approvedBy` still present in GET responses. `BcmCycleServiceTest` 18/18 (5 new), `BcmBiaAssessmentServiceTest` 16/16, suite **2,557/0/0**. The scripted annotation was verified additive — `git diff` shows **0** deleted lines across 22 files |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-26.** F2-22b stays open: 28 CRIT endpoints remain, none of them approval-provenance |
| **References** | `BcmCycleService` (`createCycle`, `createGovernance`, `requireInitialState`), `BcmBiaAssessmentService#create`, 21 entities, `MassAssignmentRatchetTest#riskProfileIsRecordedForF2_22b`, `BcmCycleServiceTest`. Same class as **DF-3**, **DF-4**, **F2-40**; found via **F2-22b**'s ranking |
| **Lesson** | **A regex under-counted a security surface for the fourth time in three days.** The annotation script matched 7 of 21 entities because it anchored `private` to the start of a line, and 14 entities write `@Column(...) private String approvedBy;` on one line. The pattern is now unmistakable in this codebase: *layout assumptions in a scan are the bug*. Second lesson, from the probe: a BIA create returned 400 and the probe called it proof, but the 400 came from `validateScope` running **before** the new guard — the same ordering trap as `@Valid`-before-`@PreAuthorize`. **A negative result only proves something if the request actually reached the code under test** |

---

## F2-22b(iv) · A due-diligence outcome could be declared without the due diligence

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-22b(iv) — fourth tranche of F2-22b |
| **Category** | Derived-value integrity / state-machine bypass / forged approval provenance |
| **Severity** | **P1 High** — three live bypasses on two compliance registers (PDPL Art. 28 processor due diligence, TPRM/SAMA outsourcing vendor assessment), all reproduced before the fix |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-28 by taking the remaining 19 CRIT rows of F2-22b's ranking and **reading each one's service** instead of annotating the list. That is what separated them: the ranking is entity-level and cannot see a service-side whitelist |
| **Current State** | The useful question for a score or an outcome is not "is it sensitive" but **does a derivation already exist elsewhere**. Reading all 19 split them three ways. **Inert (deliberately untouched):** `ClimateRiskOpportunity.riskScore` recomputes as likelihood × impact on *both* create and update; `ThirdPartyCyberAssessment.riskScore` recomputes on create and on every posture change; `NdiComplianceAssessment.complianceScore` is excluded from `update`'s whitelist and then derived by `applyRules`. **Live bypasses:** (1) `POST /api/privacy/processor-assessments` returned **201** with `status=APPROVED`, `overallOutcome=PASS`, `riskScore=1.0` and a forged `approverName`, while all eight Art. 28 controls were still `NULL` — `if (status == null) IN_PROGRESS` let every other status through, exactly as F2-22b(ii)'s BCM creates did. (2) `POST /api/v1/tprm/assessments` saved the body wholesale, minting an assessment that claimed a **REVIEWED 40-of-40 questionnaire scoring 5%** with **zero** rows in `tprm.questionnaire_responses`; `PUT` then re-asserted `riskScore`/`riskRating` over the rubric's. (3) `approverName` came from the request body on `/approve` **and the SPA sent the literal `'admin'`**, with the controller defaulting to `"admin"` when the key was absent |
| **Problem** | These are the two registers a regulator asks for by name. A processor filed as PASS with a 1.0 risk score, when not one of the eight PDPL controls was ever evaluated, is a due-diligence conclusion with no due diligence behind it. On the TPRM side the divergence was measurable rather than theoretical: a manual `PUT` moved `risk_score` 17.31 → 1.00 while `computed_score_pct` stayed 17.31, so the register and the rubric disagreed with **no trace of which was which** — a vendor could be re-banded from HIGH to LOW without touching a single answer. And every Art. 28 approval in the register read "admin" regardless of who clicked |
| **Root Cause** | Same two halves as tranche (ii): a create that treats the full status vocabulary as valid input, and derived/provenance fields the caller can set. New wrinkle: the platform *had* the derivation in both cases (`scoreAndComplete`, `QuestionnaireService.recompute`) and simply let a second, unguarded path write the same columns |
| **Business Impact** | PDPL Art. 28 and SAMA outsourcing evidence. Also the reverse: a **failing** processor assessment could not be recorded at all (F2-48), so the register could hold forged passes but not honest failures |
| **Technical Impact** | The five questionnaire-engine fields (`templateId`, `workflowStatus`, `answeredCount`, `totalQuestions`, `computedScorePct`) and four processor fields (`overallOutcome`, `riskScore`, `assessedAt`/`assessedBy`/`assessorName`, `approverName`) are `@JsonProperty(READ_ONLY)` — still serialised, asserted, per F2-22a. `create` **rejects** a non-initial status naming `/score`. `/score` and `/approve` stamp the authenticated principal. `TprmService` refuses a manual score **only** while a template owns the row. `CRIT 19 → 18` |
| **UX Impact** | `assessedBy`/`assessorName` had **no setter anywhere in the platform**, so the register's "assessed on \<date\> (\<assessor\>)" line always rendered an em dash; it now shows the real assessor. Approve/reject behave identically from the user's side |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. **`riskScore`/`riskRating` on a vendor assessment were left writable on purpose** — they are the documented "legacy free-form" columns and a manual assessment sets them by design. Refusing them outright would have been the F2-40 trap. The two TPRM rows therefore stay CRIT in the ranking, correctly. 3. The remaining 18 CRIT are the `status`/`outcome` cluster — Change, Ethics, IamJml, Cyber `maturityLevel`, NdiGap — plus the three inert score rows |
| **Alternatives** | Make `riskScore` READ_ONLY too — rejected, it breaks the manual path (F2-40's exact mistake). Convert both endpoints to DTOs — larger, and per tranche (iii) a DTO does not fix mass assignment if the DTO carries the same field |
| **Trade-offs** | An integration that created a pre-approved processor assessment now gets a 400 naming `/score`. No shipped UI does — in fact **neither raw endpoint has any UI caller at all**; the SPA drives both registers through the questionnaire engine and the transition endpoints |
| **Effort** | **S** |
| **Priority** | **Done** |
| **Verification** | Live **39/39**. Highlights: create with `status=APPROVED` → 400 naming `/score`; the forged outcome/score/attribution body → 201 with all seven fields `NULL` in the DB; the real chain derives `FAIL`/`8.0` and stamps `assessorName=admin`; `/approve` called by `compliance` with a body naming someone else records **`compliance`**; the forged questionnaire assertion lands `NOT_STARTED`/0/0/`NULL`; the manual score path still works; a questionnaire-owned override → **409** naming `/responses` with nothing written; an honest round-trip PUT still 200s; and the engine still computes end-to-end (assign-template → 8 answers → complete → review). **Browser-verified:** clicking اعتماد in the real UI recorded `approver_name='compliance'`, the logged-in user, not `'admin'` — clean console. `DerivedOutcomeBindingTest` 5/5, suite **2,582/0/0** |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-28.** F2-22b stays open: 18 CRIT remain |
| **References** | `ProcessorAssessment`, `ProcessorAssessmentService`, `ProcessorAssessmentController`, `VendorAssessment`, `TprmService#requireScoreIsNotQuestionnaireOwned`, `QuestionnaireService#upsertResponse`, `DerivedOutcomeBindingTest`, `lib/processorAssessments.ts`. Same class as **DF-3**, **DF-4**, **F2-40**, **F2-22b(ii)**; found **F2-48** |
| **Lesson** | **A derivation is only as trustworthy as its inputs.** Locking `computedScorePct` against direct assertion was worth little while the engine's own `scoredPoints` "manual override" had no bound: 5 points on a BOOLEAN question (max 1.00) drove the score to **−400.00**, which `bandFor` duly called LOW, because −400 ≤ the LOW threshold. The sanctioned path could put the number anywhere the forged path could. Found only because a probe printed a value that looked wrong and I read it instead of moving on. Second lesson: **the three inert rows matter as much as the three fixed ones** — annotating them would have claimed a fix where there was no exposure, and the honest split only came from reading nineteen services one at a time |

## F2-45 · A platform admin cannot license any organisation but its own

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-45 |
| **Category** | Multi-tenant administration / blocks a planned migration |
| **Severity** | **P3 Low** on its own, but it **blocks W2-0**, which is the gate on all of Wave 2 |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-26 while wrapping `LicensingService.upsert` for F2-22b(i). The service requires `organisationId` in its argument — the only one on the platform that does — which read as a deliberate cross-tenant admin path until the controller was read |
| **Current State** | `LicensingController.upsert` overwrites the body's tenant before delegating: `body.setOrganisationId(TenantContext.getCurrentTenant())`, commented "keeps cross-tenant writes from sneaking through". So `POST /api/v1/licensing/modules {organisationId: 2, …}` licenses **org 1**. Live-verified: the response came back `organisationId: 1` and no row was created for org 2. The service beneath it is fully cross-tenant capable |
| **Problem** | The endpoint is guarded `hasRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN')` — the platform operator, whose whole job spans tenants — and it can only ever license whichever org they are currently switched into. Licensing a customer means switching into their tenant first, one at a time, with no bulk path |
| **Root Cause** | A defensive line added against mass assignment that also removed the endpoint's reason to exist. It is the mirror image of F2-22b(i): there, a tenant guard was too narrow; here, one is too broad |
| **Business Impact** | W2-0 is specified as "**grant-all-then-tighten**" across orgs 2/3 before any `@RequireModule` annotation ships, and F2-32 records that nothing provisions licence rows at organisation creation — so every new tenant is born blacked out. Neither can be done through this API as it stands |
| **Technical Impact** | Either let `PLATFORM_ADMIN` supply `organisationId` (and rely on the F2-22b(i) opt-in scope, already added, plus the role guard), or add an explicit `POST /licensing/organisations/{orgId}/modules`. The second is better: the cross-tenant intent is in the URL, auditable, and separately guardable — a tenant admin never reaches it |
| **UX Impact** | Today a silent wrong-tenant write: the operator asks to license org 2, gets a 200, and org 1 changes. That is the worst shape of all — no error, wrong outcome |
| **Recommendation** | 1. Add the explicit org-scoped endpoint, `PLATFORM_ADMIN`-only, inside `allowCrossTenantWrite()`. 2. Until then **do not** use the existing endpoint for W2-0's backfill; a migration or the service directly. 3. Fix F2-32's provisioning gap in the same slice — a licence row set that is created at org creation is the durable answer |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Keep the stamp and drive the backfill from SQL — viable for W2-0, leaves the product gap. (b) Remove the stamp from the existing endpoint — smallest change, but silently converts a single-tenant endpoint into a cross-tenant one with no signal in the URL |
| **Trade-offs** | Any widening puts a genuine cross-tenant write in the API surface. That is what the interceptor's opt-in scope and the `PLATFORM_ADMIN` role guard are for, and the write is audited |
| **Effort** | **S** |
| **Priority** | **Medium** — sequence it immediately before W2-0 |
| **Verification** | Live 2026-07-26: `POST /api/v1/licensing/modules` with `organisationId: 2` returned a row with `organisationId: 1`, and `platform.organisation_licenses` showed no new row for org 2. Measured: orgs 2 and 3 hold exactly one licence row each (`SOCOPS ENTERPRISE`), org 1 holds all 20 `KNOWN_MODULES` |
| **Status** | ⬜ **OPEN** — deliberately not fixed inside F2-22b(i); widening who may license whom is a product decision, not a side effect of a security fix |
| **References** | `LicensingController#upsert`, `LicensingService#upsert`, `application-prod.yml` licensing block. Blocks **W2-0**; pairs with **F2-32** (no licence provisioning at org creation) and **F2-04** (entitlement rollout) |
| **Lesson** | The service and its controller disagreed about whether the operation is cross-tenant, and each was internally coherent — the service validates a field the controller has already overwritten. **Read both halves before concluding what an endpoint does**; my first version of the F2-22b(i) javadoc asserted that without the opt-in scope "module licensing would break", which was false, and only reading the controller caught it |

---

## F2-46 · The NDI compliance score reaches the regulator with nothing computing it

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-46 |
| **Category** | Fabricated metric / missing derivation — **F2-40's shape, on a regulator-facing figure** |
| **Severity** | **P1 High** — the number is in the NDI submission package, and it disagreed with the assessment it claimed to summarise |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-27 while working F2-22b's CRIT tier. `complianceScore` appeared in the risk ranking as a caller-settable field; grepping for its setter returned **nothing**, which is what made it interesting |
| **Current State** | `ndi.assessment_cycle.compliance_score` had **no setter anywhere in the codebase**. It was written only by whatever arrived in a request body, because `NdiAssessmentCycleService.create()` saves the bound entity (`update()` whitelists fields and never copied it, so create was the only way in). Four consumers read it: `NdiDashboardService` (executive tile), `NdiGovernanceDomainService` (rollup), `NdiReportService`, and **`NdiSubmissionPackageService` — the artefact submitted to the regulator**. Measured: cycle 2 asserted **68.00**; its seven child assessments average **66.43** |
| **Problem** | A compliance percentage that a caller types, flowing into a regulatory submission, with no derivation and no provenance marker — and disagreeing with the platform's own child data by 1.57 points. Nothing anywhere reconciled the two numbers, so neither the dashboard nor the submission could be checked against the assessment |
| **Root Cause** | The roll-up column was created (V-era NDI slice 1's javadoc even says "Recompute from child tables after each transition") and the recompute was never written. The same shape as F2-28 (attendance table, zero rows) and F2-40 (tally, no provenance): **the mechanism was specified and its implementation never existed** — the seventh instance |
| **Business Impact** | NDI / نضيء submissions go to SDAIA. A percentage the platform cannot derive is a number the tenant cannot defend, and the platform presents it as measured |
| **Technical Impact** | `recomputeCycleComplianceScore(cycleId)` derives the mean of the scored children, stamps `COMPUTED` + `score_computed_at`, and runs from both paths that can move a child's score (`setStatus`, `update`). `complianceScore`/`scoreSource`/`scoreComputedAt` are `@JsonProperty(READ_ONLY)` — still serialised, asserted, because all four consumers read them. Unscored children (`NOT_APPLICABLE`, `EVIDENCE_REQUIRED`, `UNDER_REVIEW` → `scoreFor` returns null) are excluded from **both** numerator and denominator, matching how the dashboard and submission package already average the column. **V323** adds the provenance columns and labels the one asserted row `ASSERTED_LEGACY` without restating it |
| **UX Impact** | The score now moves as assessors answer items, instead of sitting at whatever was typed at creation |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. ⚠ **The claim this slice can make is narrower than it first appeared** — the child scores are *also* seeded values that never passed through `scoreFor` (they read 100/55/60/50/95/65/40, while `scoreFor` yields only 100/50/0). The derivation is correct going forward; today's inputs are themselves asserted. A follow-up should decide whether to recompute the children too, which would restate a governance figure and therefore needs the same care V319/V322/V323 took. 3. `maturityScore`, `operationalExcellenceScore`, `overallScore` and `evidenceCompletionPercent` sit in the same entity and want the same audit |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Compute on read in each consumer — rejected: four consumers, four chances to diverge, and the submission package needs a persisted figure. (b) Label it and leave it asserted (F2-40's disposition) — rejected here because a derivation exists and its inputs are the assessment itself. (c) Recompute in a scheduler — rejected: the score would lag the assessment that produced it |
| **Trade-offs** | The roll-up now writes on every child status change, so a bulk assessment run does N cycle updates. At 7–200 items per cycle that is irrelevant; if a bulk-import path is ever added it should recompute once at the end |
| **Effort** | **S** |
| **Priority** | **Done** |
| **Verification** | V323 applied twice to dev, second pass identical; cycle 2's 68.00 preserved and labelled, nothing restated. Live **15/15** on a cycle the probe created (cycle 2's children are all lifecycle `APPROVED` and read-only, so mutating them to make a probe pass would have been the wrong trade): a create posting `complianceScore: 99.99` + `scoreSource` + a 2001 timestamp bound **none** of the three; one COMPLIANT child → cycle **100.00 COMPUTED** with a timestamp; adding a NON_COMPLIANT child → **50.00**, with the 5 unassessed children excluded from the denominator; `complianceScore` still serialised; **cycle 2 untouched**; cycle count back to baseline. `NdiCycleScoreProvenanceTest` 8/8, suite **2,565/0/0** |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-28** — V323 |
| **References** | `V323__ndi_cycle_score_provenance.sql`, `NdiComplianceAssessmentService#recomputeCycleComplianceScore`, `NdiAssessmentCycle`, `NdiCycleScoreProvenanceTest`. Same class as **F2-40**, **F2-28**; found via **F2-22b**'s ranking; unblocks `SECURE_CODE_REVIEW_REMEDIATION_PLAN.md` §6 |
| **Lesson** | **My first fixture was invented and the test caught it.** I asserted the children averaged 66.43 from a score set I made up (100/100/100/100/50/50/65 — which averages 80.71). Reading the real rows both fixed the test *and* revealed that the children are seeded rather than computed, which narrowed the finding's claim. **A fixture that encodes a measured number must be built from the measurement, not from a plausible reconstruction of it** — otherwise the test passes on fiction or, as here, fails and tells you your premise was wrong |

## F2-47 · An omitted `status` returned 500 instead of 400

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-47 |
| **Category** | Error handling / input validation |
| **Severity** | **P3 Low** — no data exposure; a malformed request got the wrong status code |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-28 by my own F2-46 probe sending the wrong body key. **I spent three diagnostic rounds treating the 500 as a pre-existing platform defect before reading the controller** — including disabling my own new code to prove it was not the cause, which it was not. The bug was real, just not the one I thought I had found |
| **Current State** | `NdiComplianceAssessmentController#setStatus` reads `body.get("status")`. `NdiComplianceAssessmentService.setStatus` then called `COMPLIANCE_STATUSES.contains(newStatus)` — and `COMPLIANCE_STATUSES` is an immutable `Set.of(...)`, whose `contains(null)` **throws NullPointerException** rather than returning false. So any request omitting `status`, or using a different key, produced a **500** with `detail: "NullPointerException"` and no indication of what was wrong |
| **Problem** | The caller cannot tell a malformed request from a server fault, and neither can anyone watching the 500 rate. It also makes the endpoint look broken to whoever probes it first — which is exactly what happened to me |
| **Root Cause** | `Set.of()` is null-hostile, a detail easy to miss when the value can only be null via a path the author did not expect. Compounded because every seeded NDI assessment is lifecycle `APPROVED` and therefore rejected earlier, so this endpoint had **never been exercised on a fresh row** |
| **Business Impact** | Minor in itself; matters because this is the endpoint an assessor uses to record every compliance answer in the NDI module, and it is the first thing an integration would hit |
| **Technical Impact** | Explicit null check first, and the message now names the required field and the permitted vocabulary. One line plus a test |
| **UX Impact** | A missing field now returns a 400 that says what was expected instead of a correlation reference |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. Worth a sweep: `Set.of(...).contains(x)` where `x` comes from a `Map<String,String>` body is the same trap wherever it appears |
| **Alternatives** | Switch the constants to `HashSet` — rejected: it hides the null instead of rejecting it, and immutability is worth keeping |
| **Effort** | **XS** |
| **Priority** | **Done** — fixed in passing |
| **Verification** | Live: the same call that returned 500 now returns the F2-46 probe's 200 with the correct key, and `anOmittedStatusIsABadRequestNotAServerError` pins the null path. Suite **2,565/0/0** |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-28** |
| **References** | `NdiComplianceAssessmentService#setStatus`, `NdiComplianceAssessmentController#setStatus`, `NdiCycleScoreProvenanceTest` |
| **Lesson** | **Third time this session a probe's own bug looked like a finding** (after the mutation probe that "showed" the insert guard was dead, and the BIA 400 that came from `validateScope`). The pattern is now explicit enough to state as a rule: *before believing a probe's failure is a platform defect, check the contract the endpoint actually publishes* — the controller's expected body key, the parameter name, the ordering of its validators. Disabling my own code to isolate the cause was the right instinct and it worked; reading the controller first would have been faster |

---

## F2-22b(v) · A whistleblower report could be filed already investigated and closed

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-22b(v) — fifth tranche of F2-22b, **and the one that exhausts the CRIT tier** |
| **Category** | State-machine bypass / lifecycle-evidence loss |
| **Severity** | **P1 High** — on the whistleblowing register (ISO 37002, Nazaha / HRSD pathways), reproduced live before the fix |
| **Provenance** | Found 2026-07-28 by reading all 18 remaining CRIT rows one at a time. **The headline result is the census, not the single fix:** after reading every one, **Ethics was the only real bypass left**. The others are genuinely by design or already whitelisted, and that could not be established from the ranking — only from the services |
| **Current State** | `EthicsCaseService.apply` copied `status` **and** `outcome` straight from the request body, on create *and* update. `POST /api/v1/ethics/cases {"status":"CLOSED","outcome":"UNSUBSTANTIATED"}` filed a report that was already investigated and dismissed; a `PUT` moved any case to any state at any time. The verdicts of the other 17: **inert** — `ChangeManagementController#create/#update` (CM-C3 already excludes `status` from `ChangeRequest#apply`, and `outcome` is not copied either), `NdiComplianceAssessmentController#update` (whitelist excludes `complianceScore`, then `applyRules` derives it), `ClimateRiskOpportunityController#update` and `ThirdPartyCyberAssessmentController#create` (recompute on every write); **by design** — `CyberController#save/updateControl/SubControl` (`maturityLevel` is an assessor's rating, no service derives it and the SPA posts it), `IamJmlEventController#create/#update` (a JML ingestion record whose `outcome` the source system reports — the service *reacts* to it via a prior-state snapshot), `NdiGapController#create/updateGap` (`status` is cooperative: the CAPA roll-up advances it and explicitly respects an operator's `CLOSED`/`ACCEPTED_RISK`), `CommitteeResolutionController#create` (F2-40 settled: the minute-taking form), `TprmController#create/updateAssessment` (`riskScore` is the documented free-form column, F2-22b(iv)) |
| **Problem** | Skipping the chain (INTAKE → TRIAGE → INVESTIGATION → SUBSTANTIATED/UNSUBSTANTIATED/NO_VIOLATION → REMEDIATION → CLOSED) is the lesser half. `transition()` is the **only** thing that stamps `triagedAt`, `investigationStarted`, `investigationEnded`, `closureDate` and `regulatorNotified`, so a case closed through the body carries a verdict with **no triage date, no investigation window and no closure date**. On a whistleblowing register that is indistinguishable from a report quietly made to go away — and it is the retaliation claim's own evidence that disappears |
| **Root Cause** | A field-by-field copier that treated the lifecycle columns as ordinary metadata. Identical to tranche (ii)'s BCM creates and tranche (iv)'s privacy create; **CM-C3 had already fixed exactly this on `ChangeRequest` in the same codebase**, comment and all, and nothing generalised it |
| **Business Impact** | Whistleblower case handling is the one register where the *procedure* is the control. Nazaha and HRSD ask for the investigation timeline, not the conclusion |
| **Technical Impact** | `apply` no longer copies either field. `create` rejects a non-initial status; `update` rejects a **changed** status/outcome and names the current state, the `/transition` endpoint and the legal targets from here |
| **UX Impact** | None. The SPA already drives status through `ethicsCaseApi.transition` — asserted, because the edit modal seeds from `reqFromDto(c) = { ...c }` and therefore PUTs the case's own status straight back |
| **Recommendation** | 1. As shipped. 2. **Needs your OK — 11 of 30 dev cases are stuck.** `seed_real_data.py` and `seed_gov_ethics.py` wrote `INVESTIGATING`/`TRIAGED`, which are **not in the state machine's vocabulary** (`INVESTIGATION`/`TRIAGE`), so `TRANSITIONS.getOrDefault(status, Set.of())` returns empty and *every* transition from them was already refused before this change. The PUT bypass was the only way to fix them, and it is now closed. Both seeders are corrected here (ethics rows only — the fraud module's own `INVESTIGATING` is untouched), but the existing rows need one normalising `UPDATE`, which is a data mutation and therefore yours to approve. 3. There is no CHECK constraint on `ethics.cases.status`, which is why the drift was storable at all — worth adding |
| **Alternatives** | (a) Teach `TRANSITIONS` both spellings — rejected: it bakes seed drift into the product vocabulary. (b) `READ_ONLY` on the entity, as tranches (ii)–(iv) used — rejected *here*: `status` must still be settable inbound so an integration can legitimately open a case, and a silent drop would leave a caller believing it had closed one (F2-44). (c) Keep create-with-status — see the trade-off below |
| **Trade-offs** | **A documented, tested behaviour was deliberately changed.** `EthicsCaseServiceTest.create_respects_explicit_status_severity_intake` asserted that a create supplying `status="TRIAGE"` was honoured — a case "arriving already triaged". That intent is real, but it produced a case claiming TRIAGE with no `triagedAt`. The flow still exists as create-then-transition, which yields *complete* data; the test is rewritten to state the change and its reason rather than deleted. `severity` and `intakeChannel` were never the problem and are still respected |
| **Effort** | **S** |
| **Priority** | **Done** |
| **Verification** | Live **26/26** as `compliance` (the persona holding `COMPLIANCE:WRITE`): create with `status=CLOSED` → 400 naming `/transition` *and listing the legal targets from INTAKE*; create with an `outcome` → 400 naming REMEDIATION; the honest create lands INTAKE with no verdict and no timestamps; an explicit `status=INTAKE` is accepted; `PUT status=CLOSED` → 400 with nothing written; **the SPA's edit-form echo still saves and the metadata edit lands**; the real chain runs TRIAGE → INVESTIGATION → UNSUBSTANTIATED → CLOSED and stamps all four timestamps plus the investigator; and CLOSED → INVESTIGATION is still refused. `EthicsCaseServiceTest` 24/24 (4 new), suite **2,586/0/0** |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-07-28.** **F2-22b's CRIT tier is now exhausted** — the remaining 17 are measured as inert or by design, so the row's remaining work is the 3 HIGH / 4 STATE tiers and the five DTO paths |
| **References** | `EthicsCaseService#requireLifecycleIsNotBodySet`, `#apply`, `EthicsCaseServiceTest`, `seed_real_data.py`, `seed_gov_ethics.py`. Same class as **DF-3**, **DF-4**, **F2-40**, **F2-22b(ii)**, **F2-22b(iv)**; precedent **CM-C3** |
| **Lesson** | **A ranking tells you where to look, never what is wrong.** Eighteen CRIT rows yielded one defect; the other seventeen were inert or deliberate, and every one of those verdicts required reading a service — a whitelist, a recompute call, or a comment explaining that the field is an assessor's judgement. Annotating the list would have produced seventeen fake fixes and one real one, and the fake ones would have broken working features (the F2-40 trap, four times over). Second lesson: **a fix can close the only route that repaired bad data.** The 11 stuck cases were already unadvanceable through `/transition`; the bypass was hiding it. Closing a hole means checking what was reaching through it |
