## F2-31 · The ledger every control cites as proof had no way to show it had not been edited

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-31 *(assessment round 3; shipped 2026-08-01 paired with **F2-54**, same table, one probe session)* |
| **Category** | Evidence integrity / **no tamper-evidence on the platform's own system of record** |
| **Severity** | **P1 Critical** as a compliance gap, and deliberately **not** described as a breach risk: nothing was exploited, and the honest framing is that the platform could not have *demonstrated* the absence of tampering if asked |
| **Provenance** | Round-3 review, with a caveat that turned out to be the specification: *"hash-chaining an audit ledger is near-theatre while the DBA, platform admin and deployment operator are the same person on the same SQL box… pair it with an off-box or append-only sink or state plainly that it is deterrence, not prevention."* |
| **Current State** | Before V340: `platform.audit_log` had **no chaining, no WORM, no retention policy and no protection from administrative deletion** — while `evidence.evidence_version` next door carries `content_hash_sha256` + `chain_hash_sha256`, and committee minutes are e-signed. F2-21 had already demonstrated that this ledger **will faithfully corroborate a forgery**: the record exists, so the forged action looks audited |
| **Problem** | Any actor with `UPDATE`/`DELETE` on one table could alter or remove the evidence for any control in the platform, leaving no trace. NCA ECC **2-12** requires event-log protection and ≥12-month retention; ISO 27001 A.8.15 and SAMA CSF logging say the same |
| **Root Cause** | The ledger was built as a *reporting* table (F7's "unified viewer") and later became the *evidence* table that 57 modules cite. Nothing was added when its role changed — the same drift as F2-62's `is_active` flag: a structure keeps its original guarantees while its responsibilities grow |
| **Business Impact** | A GRC platform sold on its audit trail could not answer *"prove this record has not been altered"*. In an examination, an unverifiable ledger is not neutral — it shifts every control that cites it from *evidenced* to *asserted* |
| **Technical Impact** | Four columns (`row_hash`, `prev_hash`, `hash_alg`, `sealed_at`), a periodic sealing pass, a verification walk, and two endpoints. **`hash_alg` is per row on purpose**: without it, rotating the integrity key would make every historical row fail verification, and an integrity alarm that fires on a routine operation is one operators learn to ignore |
| **UX Impact** | None yet — the verdict is API-only (`GET /api/v1/audit-log/integrity`). A screen surfacing it is deliberately out of scope until the GRC owner decides how a `PARTIALLY_VERIFIED` verdict should be presented, because presenting it badly is how an integrity report comes to mislead |
| **Recommendation** | 1. Set `AUDIT_INTEGRITY_KEY` in `external.properties` on every deployment — unkeyed, the chain is reproducible by anyone with database write access. 2. **Move key custody to someone other than the deployment operator**; that, not more code, is what converts this from deterrence to prevention. 3. Add the off-box append-only sink for tail-truncation detection (**not in this slice and not claimed**) |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **Hash on insert.** Rejected with a concrete reason: audit rows are written on every mutation across 57 modules concurrently, so two racing inserts would read the same predecessor and **fork** the chain — *a control that reports ordinary concurrency as tampering is worse than the gap it closes.* The cost of the pass instead is a sealing window, stated rather than hidden. (b) **A global cross-tenant chain.** Detects slightly more, but makes every verification a cross-tenant read, contradicting the platform's own isolation rule. (c) **Back-fill history.** Rejected — see Lesson. (d) **SQL Server ledger tables / temporal tables.** A better control, and a platform-wide DBA decision rather than a slice |
| **Trade-offs** | Rows are mutable in exactly four columns after insert, which is a departure from WORM and is why those four are excluded from the hashed content. And the sealing window is real: a row deleted before it is ever sealed leaves no trace. Both are stated in the code, the migration and the API response |
| **Effort** | **M** — hasher, service, repository queries, two endpoints, migration, 17 tests |
| **Verification** | Suite **2,744/0/0** (+17). V340 applied to dev twice, idempotent. Live **33/33** on `:8099`. Assertions are **baseline-relative**, itself a correction: demanding a literal `VERIFIED` made a correct control look broken (see below), so the probe asserts the *transition* — tamper → detected at the exact id → restore → back to exactly the baseline verdict — independently for content, link and algorithm. That is stronger than string-matching, because it proves the detector is sensitive to the field touched **and to nothing else** |
| **⚠ Both real defects were found by running the probe, not by writing the code** | **(1)** The first cut answered `verified: true` while skipping **5,000 of 6,088** rows for an algorithm mismatch — *claiming assurance over rows it had not checked, this programme's signature failure, committed inside the fix for it.* `verified` (was everything re-checked) and `tamperDetected` (did anything fail) are now separate questions, `PARTIALLY_VERIFIED` carries exact `verifiedRows`/`unverifiableRows`, and the id list is bounded instead of a 5,000-element payload. `external.properties.template` was over-reassuring in the same direction and is corrected: rotation makes old rows **unverifiable**, not verified. **(2)** `sealTenant` silently assumed every unsealed id exceeds every sealed one — true under `IDENTITY`, false the moment a historical row's integrity columns are cleared and re-offered, i.e. the tempting *"re-seal history after a rotation"* move, which would build a chain whose link order contradicts its id order and cry `LINK_BROKEN` over a ledger nobody attacked. It now refuses and logs |
| **⚠ The sealer assumes ONE instance** | Two instances sealing one tenant can fork the chain. Today's single WinSW service makes that unreachable in production; **horizontal scaling needs a shared lock first.** Not theoretical — observed here, and filed as `F2-74`: a sibling session's backend shares this tree's `target/classes`, so `mvn test` made DevTools hot-restart it into this class before it was finished, and it sealed 5,000 rows with no key into the shared dev database |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-08-01** at the level this slice claims — `V340`, commit `1cf43e95`. 🔶 **Two named follow-ons remain OPEN and are owner decisions, not code:** key custody separated from the deployment operator, and an off-box append-only sink for tail truncation. Prod applies V340 on its next restart |
| **References** | `AuditChainHasher.java` (threat boundary in the class javadoc) · `AuditChainService.java` · `AuditLogController#integrity/#seal` · `V340__audit_log_hash_chain.sql` · `AuditChainServiceTest` (17) · `external.properties.template` · `scratchpad/probe_f231c.py` |
| **Lesson** | Four. **(a) The caveat was the specification.** Round 3 said *state plainly that it is deterrence, not prevention* — so the boundary is written into the code, the migration, the template **and the API response itself**, because a limitation that lives only in a design document is not attached to the verdict an examiner reads. **(b) Back-filling would have been the defect.** Sealing pre-V340 rows produces a chain that verifies cleanly over rows that were never protected — a forged assurance, precisely what F2-21 showed this ledger would corroborate. **(c) `verified` and `tamperDetected` are different questions, and collapsing them is how an integrity control starts lying.** *Nothing to check* and *everything checked out* are different sentences; only one is evidence. **(d) The accident was more informative than the plan.** An unkeyed sibling process polluting the test database is what exposed (1) — the same shape as F2-62's *measure both databases* lesson, one layer down: there a concurrent session's **migration** corrupted a reading, here its **JVM** did |

---

## F2-61 · The export tier rule was a comparison with no direction — and read one way it re-grants what it was used to revoke

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-61 *(found closing F2-60, 2026-07-31; spec written 2026-08-01, which is when it was owed — the previous ordering pass scheduled this straight into the live queue with no register entry, breaking the rule stated in the plan's own doc-map cell)* |
| **Category** | Authorisation policy / **RC-D, enforcement switched on without re-reading the grants** — and the general case of it. A17-D10a, F2-59 and F2-60 are three symptoms; this is the cause |
| **Severity** | **P2 High** as a policy defect, with **one P1 instance** inside it. The policy half is a rule that cannot be applied without choosing between two contradictory readings. The instance half is live and was exploitable: three BCM export handlers served a principal that the same module's screens refused |
| **Provenance** | Not sought. F2-60 narrowed the regulator package 37 roles → 13 by re-homing one guard, the third time in four days that the same shape had been fixed one endpoint at a time. Three instances is where it stops being a coincidence. ➕ The measurement that produced this spec also **falsified two numbers in F2-61's own plan row**, both written the same day by me: dev/prod `VIEWER` EXPORT counts (16/17 → now **16/16**, because V335 reached production) and "granted to 32–55 roles each" (true of 6 of 26; **14 sit at ≤14 roles**) |
| **Current State** | Measured 2026-08-01 in **both** databases with `Appendix/scripts/export_grant_policy.py`, which is the deliverable half of this finding. 58 live roles, 26 `*:EXPORT` permissions catalogued, 25 granted to at least one role. Per-module relation between the `EXPORT` and `READ` holder sets: **9 conforming** (equal), **13 tighter** (EXPORT ⊂ READ), **3 inverted** (AI, BCM, BOARD). Dev and prod are now byte-identical on every one of those numbers |
| **Problem** | ADR-017 D-10 ratified *"`EXPORT` follows `READ` for the same module"*. That is a comparison **without a direction**. As a **ceiling** it means *EXPORT must not exceed READ* — narrowing. As a **floor** it means *where READ is held, EXPORT should be* — widening. Both are faithful to the words. Measured, the floor reading would add `CHANGE:EXPORT` and `REGULATORY:EXPORT` to `VIEWER` — and **`REGULATORY:EXPORT` is exactly the grant V333/V335 revoked from `VIEWER` three days earlier under this same rule.** Separately, the rule assumes every `MODULE:EXPORT` has a `MODULE:READ` to follow; `BOARD:READ` is not catalogued at all, so for 1 of 26 the rule is *unsatisfiable*, not merely unsatisfied |
| **Root Cause** | The rule was written about the **guard** side and then had to be applied to the **grant** side, where it had never been checked. `grant_tightness.py` compares guards to grants; nothing compared **two actions of the same module to each other**, which is the only axis a tier rule is about. So the platform maintained `EXPORT ⊆ READ` by hand, in a table, with no instrument — and V320 broke it in the same commit that claimed to preserve it |
| **Business Impact** | For the affected roles the **file was the only way to see the data at all**: the screen returned 403 and the download returned real bytes. That is F2-27's ratified premise — *"viewing one record does not confer extracting the register"* — running exactly backwards, on the artifact this platform sells as regulator-grade evidence. An access review reading `iam.role_permissions` would see a role with no BCM read right and conclude it cannot see continuity plans; it could take all of them, including the cycle evidence ZIP that bundles BIA, risk, strategy, gap, CAPA, compliance matrix and audit log. Under NCA ECC 2-2 and SAMA CSF 3.3.5 least privilege that is not an inconsistency, it is an unevidenced access path |
| **Technical Impact** | Three handlers authorised on `BCM:EXPORT` alone — `BcpPlanController#exportPdf`, `CrisisEventController#exportPdf`, `BcmEvidencePackageController#download`. The cause was one grant: V320 Part 3 gave `BCM:EXPORT` to `CISO` and `CFO` so that an AND-composed `hasAnyRole` list on `BcpBinderController#download` kept admitting them. **A permission is module-wide and a guard is not**, so a grant reasoned about one endpoint silently widened three others. **32 of 40** file-returning handlers still authorise on `EXPORT` alone; none is currently reachable-without-READ, and each is one grant edit from the same defect |
| **UX Impact** | None visible, and that is the property that let it live. A CISO opening the BCM workspace gets an empty/denied screen and a working download button; nothing in either surface says the two disagree about authority |
| **Recommendation** | State the rule as a **containment** — for every module, the `MODULE:EXPORT` holder set must be a **subset** of the `MODULE:READ` set — and enforce it **in the guard**, `hasPermission('M','EXPORT') and hasPermission('M','READ')`, not in the grant table. Explicitly add: `EXPORT` never *implies* a grant, so holding `READ` creates no entitlement to export |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **Revoke `BCM:EXPORT` from CISO/CFO.** Rejected: it breaks `BcpBinderController#download`, whose author explicitly listed both roles, and V320 recorded that intent. (b) **Grant `BCM:READ` to CISO/CFO.** Rejected, and this was the tempting one — it closes the asymmetry in two rows and both roles already receive strictly more as a ZIP. But it fixes an over-grant by granting, widening two executive roles across every BCM screen (crisis comms, BIA, cycles) to repair three endpoints. Wrong direction under least privilege. (c) **Sweep all 40 handlers now.** Rejected as a sweep: a module whose `EXPORT` is legitimately wider loses live access the day the AND lands, so it is a per-module decision with a probe each — F2-61(c). (d) **Gate export grants behind `PLATFORM_ADMIN`** (F2-63's suggestion). Rejected there and again here: `PLATFORM_ADMIN` bypasses permission checks in code, so that hands the sole control over export grants to the one principal whose own access is unconstrained, and moves a tenant's grant authority out to the host |
| **Trade-offs** | The `CISO`/`CFO` grant **stays in the grant table** and is now unreachable in code. A reviewer reading grants alone still sees an asymmetry — accepted deliberately, because the alternative (revoking, then re-narrowing the binder guard) reverses a recorded decision to tidy a table. It is documented in `export_grant_policy.py`'s exception list, which is where anyone measuring it will land, and the script exits non-zero on an **undocumented** asymmetry so the exception has to be argued rather than assumed |
| **Effort** | **S** for (a) + the BCM instance: one amendment, three guards, two tests, one script. **M** for (c), the 32-handler sweep |
| **Verification** | Suite **2,748/0/0** (+2 in `ExportAuthorizationRatchetTest`, which now runs 4). `export_grant_policy.py` exits **0** on dev and prod. Live on `:8084`, **11/11 before** and **11/11 after**: before — `so_officer` (CISO) 403 on `GET /api/v1/bcm/plans`, **200 + 38,043 bytes** of real `%PDF` on the same plan's `export.pdf`, **200 + 55,107 bytes** of real `PK` ZIP on the cycle evidence package; after — **403 on all three**, while `auditor`, `compliance`, `rmanager` and `viewer` (all holding both) still get 200 with real bytes on screen *and* file, and the binder guard still admits `so_officer`. **Mutation-proven**: reverting one guard to the bare form fails both new tests by handler name. ⚠ The first after-probe reported the crisis PDF still at 200 and was **right** — I had restored the guard with an editor and not recompiled, so the running process held the mutation. A probe that cannot tell you which build answered is not a probe. ➕ **(c), 2026-08-01 — suite 2,797/0/0, FE 188/188, `export_grant_policy.py` exit 0 on both databases.** The probe **manufactured** the shape instead of waiting for it: no live role was inverted for any of the 32, so a persona probe could only have shown 200-before and 200-after. A `[REVIEW-TEST]` role was given **11 `*:EXPORT` grants and no `READ` anywhere** — the exact shape V320 Part 3 produced by accident — and probed against every endpoint. **Before: 33 of 33 served 200 with real bytes** (31,404-byte risk register PDF, 40,404-byte board pack, 34,903-byte deck, 53,139-byte regulator evidence ZIP, 68,686-byte IAM access-governance ZIP, 44,995 bytes of the whole tenant calendar) while the same token was refused **403 on all seven module screens that answer** — risk, findings, changes, board packs, cyber controls, NDI reports, SGI commitments. **After: 0 of 33**, and the no-collateral claim was diffed mechanically rather than eyeballed: `so_officer`/`compliance`/`rmanager`/`viewer` unchanged in **all 164 remaining cells**. **Mutation-proven twice**: reverting one guard to the bare form fails by handler name, and reverting it *with a role list* — the form the retired ceiling accepted as “narrowed” — **still** fails. The `[REVIEW-TEST]` role and user were torn down with `CHARINDEX` deletes; residual rows re-queried at 0 |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-08-01 — all three parts.** **(a)** ADR-017 **D-10c** states the rule as a containment. **(b)** the per-module delta is `export_grant_policy.py`, re-runnable rather than a one-off report. **(c)** all **32** bare export guards now AND a `READ` permission (33 counting `/board-pack.json`), so **36 of the 38** `EXPORT`-guarded file handlers are composed and exactly **two** documented exceptions remain. ⚠ **(c) shipped as ONE code change, not the module-at-a-time revocation this row prescribes — and the prescription had the reason backwards.** Slicing is right for a *grant* revocation, where the loss has to be argued per module; moving the enforcement to the *guard* makes the collateral **provable** (`EXPORT ⊆ READ` in both databases → every principal that satisfied the old guard satisfies the new one), so there was nothing to revoke and nothing to slice. **The instrument chosen decides whether a change needs slicing at all.** ⚠ Code-only, so prod gets it on the next jar; the grant table needed no change, which is why there is nothing to Flyway |
| **References** | `ADR-017-OWNER-DELEGATED-DECISIONS.md` §D-10c · `Appendix/scripts/export_grant_policy.py` · `BcmEvidencePackageController` · `BcpPlanController#exportPdf` · `CrisisEventController#exportPdf` · `BcpBinderController#download` (the type-(ii) exception) · `MlModelController` (the AI one) · `ExportAuthorizationRatchetTest#theThreeBcmExportsAlsoDemandBcmRead`, `#exportsAuthorisedByExportAloneOnlyEverFall` · `V320__export_permission_enforcement_grants.sql` Part 3 · `scratchpad/probe_f261.py`, `probe_f261_after.py` |
| **Lesson** | Four. **(a) A rule stated as a comparison has two readings, and a policy is not finished until it says which.** *"EXPORT follows READ"* survived ratification, a council review and three implementations before anyone asked whether it narrows or widens — and the widening reading undoes a migration shipped under the narrowing one. **(b) An access-preserving claim checked in one direction is not checked.** `BcmEvidencePackageController`'s comment asserts *"all 17 roles holding BCM:READ also hold BCM:EXPORT"*, which was true; the converse was false, and it was **V320's own Part 3 that made it false, in the same file that made the claim**. Set containment has two directions and only one was measured. **(c) A grant is module-wide; a guard is not.** Granting a permission to satisfy one AND-composed endpoint widens every bare guard on that module — which is the argument for enforcing an invariant in the guard, where it cannot drift, rather than in the grant table, where it must be re-maintained after every migration. **(d) The ratchet caught its own author.** `BARE_EXPORT_CEILING` was first written as 26 from an estimate and the assertion failed at 32. A ceiling guessed high enough to pass is not a ceiling. **(e) — added closing (c). A ceiling and an invariant are different tests, and leaving the ceiling after the sweep would have been the more dangerous state.** A ceiling *tolerates* the population it measures; once the sweep lands, a tolerated guard is indistinguishable from a reviewed one. So `BARE_EXPORT_CEILING` is deleted rather than set to 0. **(f) An exemption that is easier to satisfy than the rule gets satisfied instead of the rule.** The ceiling accepted *any* `has(Any)?Role(` as sufficient narrowing, and `SecurityEventController#export` sat in that exemption with no claim to it — measured, none of its four named roles held `CYBER:EXPORT` without `CYBER:READ`, so it was excused from an invariant it already met for free. The two real exceptions are now named, not matched. **This is the third time in this programme that a scan's exemption or pattern did the work of a finding** (F2-63's missing `\s*`, F2-69's `@Entity` substring). **(g) Lesson (b) is not historical — it recurred four times in this very file's neighbourhood.** Four F2-27-era guard comments asserted access-preservation in the floor direction (*“every `READ` holder also holds `EXPORT`”*), which protects nothing, and **V335 falsified all four three days after they were written** by revoking ten `*:EXPORT` grants from `REGULATOR_VIEWER`. A claim about a grant table needs an owner that re-measures it, or it is a comment with a shelf life |

## F2-80 · Twenty-two denial messages are written and none is delivered — including the segregation-of-duty gate

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-80 *(found 2026-08-01 while live-probing A17-D8; spec written the same day)* |
| **Category** | Error contract / observability — with a **disclosure trap in the obvious fix**. Not an authorisation hole: the discard is fail-safe in the security direction and unsafe in the usability one |
| **Severity** | **P3 Medium.** Nothing is reachable that should not be. What is lost is every explanation the code wrote for a refusal, on exactly the flows where the user cannot guess the answer |
| **Provenance** | Not sought, and found by being misled. During the A17-D8 before-probe, `admin` — `PLATFORM_ADMIN`, **named explicitly** in `hasAnyRole('SOC_TIER_2','INCIDENT_COMMANDER','CISO','PLATFORM_ADMIN')` — returned **403** on `POST /crowdstrike/contain/{hostId}`, as did every other persona. That reads as a broken guard. It was `CrowdStrikeOutboundService`'s SEC-INT-003 object-level check (*“Falcon host is not in this tenant's inventory”*) firing on a fabricated host id, rendered identically to an authorisation refusal. The guard was fine; the probe could not tell |
| **Current State** | `GlobalExceptionHandler:154` — `handleAccessDenied` builds its ProblemDetail with a **literal** string and never reads `ex.getMessage()`. Measured by script over `src/main/java`: **22 of 22** hand-thrown `new AccessDeniedException(…)` sites pass a message, and **22 of 22** are discarded. They span 10 services in 8 modules: `admin/committee` (3), `attachment` (3), `bcm` (4), `change_mgmt` (3), `policy` (3), `workflow/approval_chains` (2), `approvals` (1), `signature` (1), `integrations` (2) |
| **Problem** | The response the caller receives is always *“You do not have permission to perform this action.”* For a genuine permission gap that is correct. For the 22 it is wrong in a specific way: it **misattributes the cause**. A policy owner refused at `PolicyManagementService:413` has the right permission — the refusal is *“Segregation of duty: the policy author/owner cannot approve their own policy”*, which is the control working. The user is told to go and get access they already hold |
| **Root Cause** | Two 403 paths with **opposite** message policies, and the wrong one is the default for domain refusals. `handleBusinessRule` passes `ex.getMessage()` through and its own comment states the principle — *“403 with the rule's own message, since explaining the rule is the point”*. `AccessDeniedException` is Spring's type for framework denials, where a generic message is right; the 22 sites reached for it because a domain refusal *is* a 403, and inherited a policy written for a different sender |
| **Business Impact** | The four-eyes and maker-checker gates are what this platform sells against NCA ECC 2-2 and SAMA CSF 3.3.5, and they are the ones that need an explanation most: a CAB decision refused for want of `CHANGE:APPROVE`, a committee vote refused because the caller is not an active member, a plan-distribution acknowledgement refused because the caller is not the recipient. Each throws a message naming the alternative route, and each surfaces as a flat permission error. Support cost is the visible form; **the invisible form is a customer concluding a control is a bug** |
| **Technical Impact** | An authorisation probe cannot distinguish *“you may not”* from *“that record is not yours”* — both are 403 with the same body. This platform's own review programme probes authorisation continuously, so the ambiguity taxes the instrument it is verified with. It cost this slice one false lead directly |
| **UX Impact** | The user-facing half. `problemMessage()` folds `detail` into the toast, so the toast is only ever as good as `detail`; here `detail` is a constant. **Identical in shape to the *“dead buttons”* reported during the green-finance work**, which resolved to bare *“Validation Failed”* toasts with the real cause discarded upstream. That was fixed in one handler; this is the same defect in its neighbour |
| **Recommendation** | Move the 22 domain denials onto the `BusinessRuleException` path (or a dedicated `DomainAccessDeniedException` that the handler treats as message-bearing), **reviewing each message for what it discloses**, and leave `handleAccessDenied` generic for everything Spring itself throws. Add a test asserting no `new AccessDeniedException(` with a string literal exists outside the framework boundary — otherwise the 23rd is written next month |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **Pass `ex.getMessage()` through in `handleAccessDenied`.** Rejected — one line, and it introduces a disclosure: `PlanDistributionService:152` interpolates `row.getRecipientName()` and `CommitteeResolutionService:210` interpolates `seat.getMemberName()`, so an unauthorised caller would learn a colleague's name from a 403. It would also echo Spring's own denial text for `@PreAuthorize` failures. (b) **Add a `reason` property and leave `detail` generic.** Viable and half-hearted: the FE would need to prefer `reason` over `detail`, which is the same work with an extra field. (c) **Return 404 for object-scope misses.** Rejected: it fixes the probe ambiguity and leaks existence, the trade SEC-INT-003 deliberately declined |
| **Trade-offs** | Message-bearing 403s are an information-disclosure surface by construction. The gain is that a refusal explains itself; the cost is that each message must be written for an audience that may be hostile. That is a per-message review, which is why this is not a one-line change |
| **Effort** | **M** — 22 call sites, a handler split, one ratchet, and a per-message disclosure review. No migration; no schema |
| **Verification** | Not yet fixed. The measurement is reproducible: count `new AccessDeniedException(` sites with a string argument under `src/main/java` (**22**), and confirm `handleAccessDenied` never references `ex.getMessage()`. Live: `POST /api/v1/integrations/crowdstrike/contain/NOSUCHHOST` as `admin` returns 403 with `detail` = *“You do not have permission to perform this action.”* while the log line reads *“host … is not in tenant 1's inventory”* — the server knows and does not say |
| **Status** | ⬜ **OPEN.** Filed from A17-D8, not fixed there: re-pointing 22 exception throws across 8 modules is its own slice with its own probe set, and folding it into a permission-catalogue change would have made both unreviewable |
| **References** | `common/exception/GlobalExceptionHandler.java:154` (`handleAccessDenied`) and `:165` (`handleBusinessRule`, the correct policy) · `PolicyManagementService:413` (the SoD gate) · `ChangeManagementService:706` · `CommitteeResolutionService:198`, `:210` · `PlanDistributionService:152` · `CrowdStrikeOutboundService:121` · `TenableOutboundService:332` · [[F2-80]] in `UNIFIED_IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md` § findings |
| **Lesson** | **A message that is written but not delivered is worse than no message, because the code reads as if the user was told.** Three reviewers looked at `PolicyManagementService:413` during the Change+Policy remediation and read a well-worded SoD refusal; none checked which handler would render it. The general form: **an error contract is a property of the handler, not of the throw site**, and every throw site in this codebase was written as though it were the other way round |


## F2-45 · The licence endpoint answered 200 for the wrong organisation

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-45 *(found by F2-22b(i), 2026-07-26; spec written 2026-08-01, the day it was worked — it had lived only inside W2-0's plan row)* |
| **Category** | Authorisation / API contract — a tenant-stamping guard applied to the one endpoint whose tenant is legitimately caller-supplied |
| **Severity** | **P3 Medium.** No unauthorised access: the only caller is `PLATFORM_ADMIN` and the write stayed inside their own tenant. The harm is a false belief about a commercial fact |
| **Provenance** | Not sought. F2-22b measured body-supplied `organisationId` across all **214** entity-bound writes to find mass-assignment sites; licensing came back as the single legitimate one, and the measurement then showed the controller discarding it |
| **Current State** | `LicensingController.upsert` opened with `body.setOrganisationId(TenantContext.getCurrentTenant())`, commented *"keeps cross-tenant writes from sneaking through"*. `disable` used `TenantContext` only and took no organisation at all |
| **Problem** | The overwrite kept nothing out — `PLATFORM_ADMIN` switches tenants at will, and `LicensingService.upsert` already declares `TenantContext.allowCrossTenantWrite()`. What it did was **silently retarget the write**: a body naming organisation 2 returned **200 OK with a row for organisation 1**. The operator's belief that tenant 2 was licensed was wrong and nothing in the response said so. *A 403 would have been safe; succeeding against the wrong tenant is the failure mode a customer discovers* |
| **Root Cause** | A platform-wide rule — *stamp the tenant from `TenantContext`, never trust the body* — applied to the **one** endpoint it does not fit. Licensing is inherently cross-tenant: a host operator names the organisation being licensed. The rule is right 213 times out of 214, and the exception was never written down, so a later reader hardened the exception |
| **Business Impact** | It is the mechanism W2-0's grandfather backfill would have used, and the reason the backfill had to be driven from a migration instead. Operationally: a tenant believed to be licensed is not, and the first symptom is a 403 on a module the customer has paid for — after F2-04 annotates ~2,138 endpoints, on any module |
| **Technical Impact** | Two endpoints. `upsert` retargeted silently; `disable` could not name another organisation at all, so grant and revoke had different reach — an operator could create a cross-tenant row (via the service) and not remove it |
| **UX Impact** | The worst kind: none. 200 OK, a well-formed body, and the `organisationId` in the response echoing the value the server substituted rather than the one sent |
| **Recommendation** | Honour `body.organisationId` when supplied, default to the caller's tenant when not, and **verify the organisation exists** — `organisation_licenses` has no foreign key to `platform.organisations`, so an unchecked id writes rows nothing will ever read. Give `disable` the same optional parameter |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **Refuse a body naming another organisation (400).** Honest, and leaves the backfill without an API. (b) **Add a separate `/organisations/{id}/licences` sub-resource.** Cleaner REST, and a second role-guarded endpoint the F2-05 ratchet would count. (c) **Leave it and document it.** Rejected: the defect is a silent wrong answer, and documenting a silent wrong answer does not make it loud |
| **Trade-offs** | The endpoint is now genuinely cross-tenant, so its correctness rests on `hasRole('PLATFORM_ADMIN')` and on `allowCrossTenantWrite()` being scoped to this one service method. Both were already true; this change makes them load-bearing. A cross-tenant licence **read** was deliberately not added — a `PLATFORM_ADMIN` reads any tenant's licences by switching into it, so a new endpoint would raise the role-guard count to save a round trip |
| **Effort** | **S** — two controller methods, one service method, 2 unit tests, live probe |
| **Verification** | Suite **2760/0/0**. Live **28/28** on `:8084`: a body naming org 2 returns 200 with `organisationId: 2`, **org 2's row changes and org 1's does not**; org 999 is refused **404** and writes nothing; omitting `organisationId` still targets the caller's tenant; `disable?organisationId=2` disables org 2 and leaves org 1 enabled; `viewer` is **403** on both writes and still reads its own tenant's licences |
| **Status** | ✅ **CLOSED 2026-08-01** with W2-0 (branch `feat/w2-0-licence-backfill`). Code-only — prod gets it on the next jar |
| **References** | `LicensingController#upsert`, `#disable` · `LicensingService#resolveLicensingTarget` · `LicensingServiceTest#resolveLicensingTarget_*` · [[F2-22b]] (the 214-write measurement) · W2-0 in `UNIFIED_IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md` §3 |
| **Lesson** | **A platform-wide invariant needs its exceptions written down, or the next reader will harden the exception.** *"Never trust the body's tenant"* is correct on 213 of 214 writes, and applying it to the 214th produced a wrong answer rather than a refusal — which is strictly worse, because a refusal is visible |

---

## F2-81 · The platform cannot onboard a tenant — nothing creates an organisation

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-81 *(measured while building W2-0, 2026-08-01)* |
| **Category** | Product completeness / multi-tenancy — the commercial kind, not the isolation kind |
| **Severity** | **P2 High** commercially, **P4** technically. Nothing is broken; something is absent, and it is the thing a SaaS contract assumes |
| **Provenance** | Not sought. W2-0's plan row said *"nothing creates licence rows on organisation creation"*, so the fix looked like a provisioning hook. Looking for the creation to hook into is what found there is none |
| **Current State** | `OrganisationController` exposes `GET /accessible` and `POST /switch/{id}` — **no POST to create.** `OrganisationRepository` is a bare `JpaRepository<Organisation, Long>` with one finder, and **no code anywhere calls `save` on it**: all eight consumers read a name for an export header. Organisations 1–3 exist because the Python bootstrap inserted them |
| **Problem** | F2-32's closing sentence presumes a creation path to fix. There is none — so there is nothing to provision licence rows, roles, a first administrator, branding, or an audit record of who onboarded whom. **A platform whose tenant count can only be changed by a DBA is not multi-tenant in the commercial sense**, whatever the code does with `organisation_id` |
| **Root Cause** | The tenant model was built from the inside out: `TenantContext`, per-query filtering, a switcher and a write-guard interceptor all exist and work. The *entry* was never built, because dev never needed one — the bootstrap script created the tenants the demo required, and each new tenant's licence row was hand-written into whichever migration was open at the time (`SOCOPS` for orgs 2 and 3, in V150 and later). **The workaround is what kept the gap invisible** |
| **Business Impact** | Onboarding a customer requires a DBA with `sa` on the production database, and the steps are not written down in one place. Under SAMA outsourcing and NCA ECC change-control expectations, provisioning a tenant by ad-hoc SQL is not an auditable process; and a self-service trial, a reseller, or any multi-tenant sales motion is impossible today |
| **Technical Impact** | Every downstream default is unowned. After F2-04 annotates ~2,138 endpoints against licence rows, a hand-inserted organisation is **fail-closed on everything**, which is a support incident that looks like a platform outage |
| **UX Impact** | There is no screen. A platform administrator can switch between organisations and cannot create one |
| **Recommendation** | One transactional onboarding operation — organisation → first administrator → role grants → branding defaults → the 15 licence rows → an audit event — with a rollback if any step fails, because a half-provisioned tenant is worse than none. Belongs with F2-04 or immediately after it, since F2-04 is what makes the licence half load-bearing |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **A documented runbook + SQL script.** Cheapest, auditable if the script is versioned, and still needs `sa`. (b) **A provisioning hook on `OrganisationRepository.save`.** Rejected — a hook on a method nobody calls is speculative code that will be wrong by the time it has a caller. (c) **What this slice did:** grandfather the tenants that exist (V344) and make the fourth one's absence **loud** — `licence_coverage.py` exits non-zero on any tenant missing any ratified SKU |
| **Trade-offs** | Detection instead of prevention. The next organisation will still be born blacked out; the difference is that a script now says so in one line instead of a customer saying so in a ticket |
| **Effort** | **M** — a feature, not a fix: endpoint, service, transaction boundary, tests, and a decision about who may create a tenant |
| **Verification** | The absence is verifiable: `grep -rn "OrganisationRepository" --include=*.java` returns 8 consumers and **0** `save` calls; `OrganisationController` has no `@PostMapping` other than `switch/{id}`. Coverage of the tenants that do exist: `py Appendix/scripts/licence_coverage.py --db TSS_GRC` exits **0**; the same against prod exits **1** until V344 runs there |
| **Status** | ⬜ **OPEN.** V344 + `licence_coverage.py` shipped 2026-08-01 as the interim; the onboarding path is unscheduled |
| **References** | `module/platform/controller/OrganisationController.java` · `module/platform/repository/OrganisationRepository.java` · `V97__module_licensing.sql` (the `organisation_id = 1` literal) · `Appendix/scripts/licence_coverage.py` · [[F2-32]] · W2-0, [[F2-04]] |
| **Lesson** | **A seed written for one tenant is a provisioning bug that only surfaces when there is a second tenant** — and it will surface as an entitlement failure, not as a missing feature, because the missing feature has no error message. Also: *the plan's description of a defect is a hypothesis about its cause.* This row exists because the stated cause ("no licence rows on creation") was checked instead of assumed |

---

## F2-82 · Three entity field defaults silently overwrote every partial licence update

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-82 *(found probing W2-0's own fix, 2026-08-01)* |
| **Category** | Entity-as-DTO / partial-update semantics. **Not** the mass-assignment shape F2-30 ratchets — no attacker sets a field they should not; a *default* overwrites a value nobody sent |
| **Severity** | **P3 Medium**, with a commercial edge: the overwritten values are contract terms, and one of them is an enforcement flag |
| **Provenance** | Not sought, and found only because the live probe printed the whole row rather than the field under test. The F2-45 probe re-enabled a licence and the assertion passed; the printed row showed `tier` had changed from `ENTERPRISE` to `PRO` |
| **Current State** | `OrganisationLicense` doubles as the `@RequestBody` and initialised `tier = "PRO"`, `validFrom = LocalDate.now()`, `enabled = true`. `LicensingService.upsert`'s update branch is `if (body.getX() != null) existing.setX(body.getX())` |
| **Problem** | A defaulted field is **never null**, so all three fired on every partial update. Measured live: `{"enabled": true}` on one tenant's `ESG` row rewrote **ENTERPRISE → PRO**. A patch to `notes` resets `validFrom` — the licence start date — to today. And **any unrelated edit re-enables a module the business deliberately disabled**, which undoes the only commercial control `organisation_licenses` carries |
| **Root Cause** | Two correct-looking idioms that are incompatible: a JPA entity with sensible field defaults, and a partial update that treats `null` as *not supplied*. Together, *not supplied* and *supplied as the default* become the same value, and the second wins. **The create branch already defaulted all three behind `== null` checks that could never fire** — dead code that proves its author expected nulls, and that nobody reconciled with the initialisers |
| **Business Impact** | De-licensing is how the platform stops serving an unpaid module. If any subsequent edit re-enables it, the control is advisory. Tier downgrades and reset start dates are silent losses of contract terms in the table an invoice would be reconciled against |
| **Technical Impact** | Every write through `POST /api/v1/licensing/modules` after a `disable` re-opens the module for the tenant, cache eviction and all — the JWT claim picks it up on the tenant's next login, so the change propagates without anyone acting |
| **UX Impact** | Silent. 200 OK, and the response body shows the overwritten values, so a UI reading the response renders the wrong state as if it were the state the operator asked for |
| **Recommendation** | Remove the initialisers; keep the defaults in the create branch, where they already were. Assert the preservation against values a default would **change** |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **A separate patch DTO with `Optional<T>` fields.** Correct and larger; F2-22b records this endpoint's entity-as-body as deliberate, so it would be a wider change than the defect. (b) **`@DynamicUpdate` / JSON-merge-patch.** Same conclusion. (c) **Keep the initialisers, compare against them.** Rejected — "if the value equals the default, assume it was not sent" makes it impossible to *set* a licence to PRO |
| **Trade-offs** | `tier`, `validFrom` and `enabled` are `nullable = false` in the schema, so a create path that forgets to default them now fails at the database rather than silently inserting `PRO`/today/`true`. That is the correct direction, and the create branch already covers all three |
| **Effort** | **S** — three field initialisers, 5 unit tests |
| **Verification** | Suite **2760/0/0** (+5 in `LicensingServiceTest`, 7 → 12). **Mutation-proven**: reinstating `enabled = true` fails `upsert_partialUpdateDoesNotResurrectDefaults` by name with the de-licensing message. Live before/after: the same re-enable request left `tier` at `ENTERPRISE` after the fix, where it had produced `PRO` before |
| **Status** | ✅ **FIXED 2026-08-01** with W2-0. Code-only |
| **References** | `OrganisationLicense` (`tier`, `validFrom`, `enabled`) · `LicensingService#upsertInternal` · `LicensingServiceTest#upsert_partialUpdateDoesNotResurrectDefaults` · [[F2-30]] (the mass-assignment ratchet this is adjacent to and not an instance of) · [[F2-45]] |
| **Lesson** | Two. **(a) A test that asserts a field still equals the value the bug would also produce is not testing anything.** `upsert_existingRowUpdatesFields` claimed *"existing fields preserved"* and passed for years: it patched a row whose `enabled` was already `true`, so preservation and overwriting were indistinguishable. The replacement starts the row DISABLED, on FREE, dated last year — three values no default produces. **(b) Print the whole row, not the field under test.** This was found because a passing assertion sat next to a printed row that had quietly changed in a column nobody was asserting |


## F2-83 · Every anonymous whistleblower report and consumer complaint is filed into organisation 1

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-83 *(measured while implementing F2-04, 2026-08-01)* |
| **Category** | Multi-tenancy / confidentiality — **not** authorisation. No guard is missing; the row is written to the wrong tenant |
| **Severity** | **P1 Critical** on the confidentiality of two channels a regulator mandates. Latent on a single-host deployment, structural the moment a second customer gets their own portal |
| **Provenance** | Found because F2-04 annotates controllers at class level, and four controllers mix `permitAll` endpoints in with authenticated ones. Asking "can these anonymous endpoints be entitlement-checked?" required knowing which tenant they belong to — and they do not know |
| **Current State** | `WhistleblowerCaseService.PUBLIC_PORTAL_DEFAULT_ORG = 1L` and `ComplaintService.PUBLIC_PORTAL_DEFAULT_ORG = 1L`, both reached through `resolvePublicOrg() { return TenantContext.getCurrentTenant() != null ? … : PUBLIC_PORTAL_DEFAULT_ORG; }`. Both carry a TODO naming host-based resolution as the intended fix |
| **Problem** | For a genuine anonymous caller there is no JWT, so `getCurrentTenant()` is null and **the fallback is the only path, not an edge case**. Live-verified with four organisations present: three whistleblower submissions and three complaints, all posted anonymously, all written to `organisation_id = 1`. The complaint rows carry `customer_name`, `customer_phone`, `customer_email` and an ID-document type; the whistleblower rows carry `reporter_name`/`email`/`phone` whenever the reporter is not anonymous |
| **Root Cause** | The same shape as [[F2-81]] and, before it, `V97`'s literal `organisation_id = 1`: **a single-tenant assumption written as a constant, in a platform that later grew tenants.** The nearest thing to a workaround hid it — with one host serving the SPA there is only one portal, so org 1 is also the right answer, and it stays right until the day it silently is not |
| **Business Impact** | Bank B's employee reports misconduct on Bank B's portal and the case lands in Bank A's tenant: Bank A's ethics officer can list it, Bank B's cannot see their own report. **Nazaha**, SAMA CG Art.28 and CMA CG Art.87 require a *confidential* reporting channel; filing a report into a different legal entity is a confidentiality failure, not a routing bug. SAMA Consumer Protection Principles carry the same weight for complaints, plus the PII |
| **Technical Impact** | Blocks entitlement on these four controllers: a licence check needs a tenant, so the anonymous half of each is exempt in `LicenseGuardAspect` — recorded there and deliberately, not silently. That produces a visible asymmetry (a *logged-in* caller on the same endpoint **is** entitlement-checked) which disappears once tenancy resolves |
| **UX Impact** | None visible to the reporter — they receive a case code and a token, and tracking works, because status/reply resolve the org from the row rather than the fallback. **Only the creation path is wrong**, which is why nothing surfaced it |
| **Recommendation** | **The platform already solved this, once.** `SubprocessorController.publicList` (SEC-PRIV-003) takes an opaque non-enumerable `org` short code, requires it to exist and be active, returns 404 otherwise, and never falls back to org 1 — it was hardened precisely because it *had* trusted a numeric id defaulting to 1. Carry that pattern to both portals: the public submit takes the tenant's short code (or a host-derived resolution), and an unknown/inactive code is refused rather than defaulted. Then delete both constants |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **Host-based tenant resolution** (`ethics.<bank>.sa`) — what both TODOs propose, and the right long-term answer; larger, since nothing resolves a tenant from a host today. (b) **Keep the default and document it** — rejected: it is a confidentiality control, and "documented" does not route the report. (c) **Refuse anonymous submission until tenancy is resolvable** — rejected: taking a regulator-mandated channel offline is worse than mis-filing within one deployment |
| **Trade-offs** | Making the short code required is a breaking change for any existing public portal link, and there is exactly one deployment to update. Doing it *with* [[F2-81]]'s onboarding path is cheaper than doing it twice |
| **Effort** | **S** for the short-code pattern (it exists and is 20 lines away); **M** with host resolution |
| **Status** | ⬜ **Open.** Measured, evidenced and recorded; the entitlement exemption it forces is documented in `LicenseGuardAspect.isAnonymousHttpRequest` so the asymmetry cannot be mistaken for an oversight |
| **References** | `WhistleblowerCaseService#resolvePublicOrg` · `ComplaintService#resolvePublicOrg` · `SubprocessorController#publicList` (the pattern to copy) · `SecurityConfig` lines 130–140 · `LicenseGuardAspect#isAnonymousHttpRequest` · [[F2-81]] · [[F2-04]] |
| **Lesson** | **A fix applied to one endpoint is not a pattern until it is applied to the others that share the flaw.** SEC-PRIV-003 fixed exactly this defect — client-supplied numeric org defaulting to 1 — in `privacy`, and left it in the two places where the data is most confidential. The same pilot-without-rollout shape as F2-04 itself, one layer down |


## F2-84 · The entitlement guard had never returned 403 — every denial was a 500

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-84 *(found by F2-04's own live probe, 2026-08-01)* |
| **Category** | Error contract / exception-handler precedence |
| **Severity** | **P2 High.** The platform's answer to "you have not bought this module" was `500 Internal Server Error` |
| **Provenance** | The probe asserted 403 on an unlicensed module and got 500. The mechanism was then found already documented **three handlers away**, for a different exception |
| **Current State** | `ModuleDisabledException` carries `@ResponseStatus(HttpStatus.FORBIDDEN)` and had **no explicit handler**. `GlobalExceptionHandler` is a `@RestControllerAdvice` ending in `@ExceptionHandler(Exception.class)` |
| **Problem** | Spring's `ResponseStatusExceptionResolver` — which honours `@ResponseStatus` — runs **after** `@RestControllerAdvice`, so the catch-all claimed the exception first and returned `500` with `type=errors/internal`. Live-verified: `GET /api/v1/bcm/dependencies` as a tenant holding `BCM_READ` but no `BCM` licence → `500`, twice, on two different controllers |
| **Root Cause** | The identical hijack is described in this same file, in the javadoc of `handleResponseStatus`: *"Spring's built-in `ResponseStatusExceptionResolver` runs AFTER this `@RestControllerAdvice`, so without an explicit handler the catch-all `handleGeneral` hijacks the exception and mis-reports a deliberate 4xx as a 500."* **It was written for `ResponseStatusException` and never generalised to the other exception in the codebase that leans on `@ResponseStatus`** |
| **Why it stayed invisible** | It was **unreachable**. Entitlement covered only `socops`; every tenant holds `SOCOPS`; there are **no socops controller tests** and the suite boots no web context (4 MockMvc tests, 0 `@SpringBootTest`). So the guard's *failure* path had never executed — not in a test, not in dev, not in production. F2-04's sweep from 22 to 197 controllers is what made it reachable |
| **Business Impact** | In **prod**, SEC-CFG-001 strips the exception class and root message, so a customer who simply has not licensed a module receives a bare `Internal Server Error` and a correlation id: indistinguishable from an outage. The support path becomes a bug report instead of a sales conversation, and the "add Privacy later" upsell F2-04 exists to enable would have surfaced as a platform fault |
| **Technical Impact** | Falsified three written claims at once: `ModuleDisabledException`'s own javadoc (*"Mapped to HTTP 403 Forbidden so the frontend can distinguish licensing failures"*), ADR-013, and `SKU_CATALOGUE.md` §1 (*"Server-denied when unlicensed"*). The frontend's `problemMessage()` had no `module-not-licensed` case to key on because no response ever carried one |
| **Recommendation** | An explicit `@ExceptionHandler(ModuleDisabledException.class)` returning 403 with `type=https://grc.tss.com/errors/module-not-licensed` and **`moduleCode` as a structured property**, so the caller need not guess which SKU is missing — the [[F2-80]] shape, avoided |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **Reorder the resolvers.** Rejected: it would change the status of every `@ResponseStatus` exception at once, which is a platform-wide behaviour change to fix one contract. (b) **Have the aspect throw `ResponseStatusException`.** Works, and loses the typed `moduleCode`/`organisationId` the frontend needs. (c) **Exclude `ModuleDisabledException` from the catch-all.** Same effect, less explicit, and the next such exception repeats the bug |
| **Trade-offs** | None found. The handler is additive and the 500 it replaces was never intended |
| **Effort** | **S** — one handler, 3 tests |
| **Verification** | Live before/after on the same URL and tenant: `500 errors/internal` → `403 errors/module-not-licensed`, detail *"Your organisation is not licensed for the BCM module."* Probe **14/14**, with a control group (the same URLs as a tenant holding `BCM` → 200, so the 403 is the licence and not a broken endpoint). **Mutation-proven**: removing the handler fails 2 of 3 `ModuleDisabledStatusTest` tests with 500 |
| **Status** | ✅ **FIXED 2026-08-01** with F2-04a. Code-only, no migration |
| **References** | `GlobalExceptionHandler#handleModuleDisabled` · `GlobalExceptionHandler#handleResponseStatus` (the javadoc that already explained it) · `ModuleDisabledException` · `ModuleDisabledStatusTest` · [[F2-04]] · [[F2-80]] |
| **Lesson** | Two. **(a) A control whose failure path never executes is not a control that works — it is a control nobody has run.** Entitlement had been "in production for one module" since F9, and its denial had never once been observed, because every tenant satisfied it. *Coverage of the success path told us nothing about the contract.* **(b) When a codebase documents a framework trap, grep for the other things that step in it.** The precedence bug was written down, accurately, for a sibling exception — and the one other exception with the same annotation was two files away |


## F2-87 · Changes were approved on behalf of a committee the tenant never named — and the platform had been WARNing about it at every boot for a week

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-87 *(measured while implementing ADR-017 **A17-D2**, 2026-08-01)* |
| **Category** | Authorisation of a governance decision — **not** a permission gap. Every caller held `CHANGE:APPROVE`; the missing thing was the *body* the decision is attributed to |
| **Severity** | **P1 Critical** on change-approval evidence. Two of three tenants, every CAB- and ECAB-routed change |
| **Provenance** | A17-D2 was queued as "spine work" whose prerequisite was an unbuilt tenant-config surface. Measuring first showed the surface exists (V310, seeded V314) and that the **rule for its absence** — D-2's actual decision — was the thing missing |
| **Current State (before)** | `change_authorisation_matrix` demands `CAB` on 5 cells and `ECAB` on 6 in **all three** organisations. `admin.committee_gate_map` had rows for **org 1 only**. `ChangeManagementService.transition` applied the committee gate **only when a referral already existed**, and a referral cannot be created for an unmapped gate (`CommitteeReferralService.refer` throws), so orgs 2 and 3 always took the `else` branch — the legacy recorded-vote tally |
| **Problem** | Live-probed on the running stack in organisation 2 (11 matrix cells demanding a committee, **zero committees**, no gate-map row): a `NORMAL/LOW` change → `POST /transition {"target":"CAB_APPROVED"}` → **200**, satisfied by one legacy vote row and **no committee**; an `EMERGENCY/LOW` change → `EMERGENCY_APPROVED` → **200 from `DRAFT` with zero recorded votes and no committee**. `cabDecisionAt` is stamped on both paths, so the row then *claims* a CAB decision that no CAB took. ⚠ **A second, narrower hole in the same predicate:** a gate-map row pointing at a **DISSOLVED** committee made `governance-health` stop reporting the gap (`unmapped` went from `[CAB, ECAB]` to `[ECAB]`) while `refer()` still rejected that committee — the report and the enforcement disagreed |
| **Root Cause** | **F-4 was closed by making the gap loud, and a WARN is not a control.** `ChangeGovernanceHealthService` was added for exactly this state and its own javadoc says *"in practice every change quietly falls back to the legacy approval tally"* — accurate, shipped, and logged at every boot since V314: *"4 authority gate(s) demand a committee but none is mapped … will fall back to the legacy approval tally"*. Nothing consumed it. The platform described the defect to itself 48 times a day |
| **Business Impact** | ITIL 4 change enablement, SAMA CSF 3.3.5 and NCA ECC-2 all require **documented change authority**. An approval recorded against a CAB that does not exist is not a weaker control — it is fabricated governance evidence, the failure mode this programme has paid for four times. In an examination the register would show authorised changes whose authorising body cannot be produced |
| **Technical Impact** | The `gate` column of the authorisation matrix was **advisory** — a tenant could set CAB on every cell and it changed nothing unless somebody separately called `refer-to-committee` per change |
| **Recommendation** | Fail closed on the **permissive** transitions (`CAB_APPROVED`, `EMERGENCY_APPROVED`) with an actionable 409, and share the "is this gate configured?" predicate with the health report so the two cannot drift. **Not** `CAB_REJECTED`: refusing a *rejection* because no committee exists keeps alive a change somebody tried to kill — fail-closed means refusing the transition that grants authority, not every transition that mentions the CAB |
| **Alternatives** | (a) **Require a committee decision for every CAB-gated change, abolishing the legacy tally.** Rejected for this slice: it is a migration of live governance data, and D-2 decides the *unset* case only. (b) **Auto-create a committee per tenant.** Rejected by V314's own reasoning — a body with no charter, chair or members can never reach quorum, so the gate would fail at the last step while looking configured. (c) **Keep warning.** Rejected: this finding *is* what happens to a warning |
| **Trade-offs** | A tenant that has not named its CAB loses the ability to approve CAB-routed changes — deliberately, and only after the platform has been telling them for a week. Mitigated by shipping the screen that fixes it in the same change |
| **Effort** | **S** for the gate; **S** for the config surface it must name |
| **Verification** | Live before/after, same tenant and same URLs. **Before:** `CAB_APPROVED` 200, `EMERGENCY_APPROVED` 200, dissolved-committee case 200 and health reporting `[ECAB]` only. **After:** `409 change-authority-not-configured` `reason=NOT_MAPPED` on both, `reason=COMMITTEE_UNAVAILABLE` on the dissolved case, health reporting `[CAB, ECAB]`, and the DB row **unmoved** in every refusal. Controls: `CAB_REJECTED` 200 before and after; organisation 1 (configured) 200 before and after. **Round trip through the product:** create committee → `PUT /api/v1/changes/authority/CAB` → same transition **200** → `DELETE` → **409** again. `PUT` as a `CHANGE:APPROVE` persona without `ADMIN:WRITE` → **403**. Suite **2807/0/0**, FE **188/188**. **Mutation-proven three ways:** with the health dependency inert 3 of 10 tests fail; removing `c.status <> 'DISSOLVED'` from the shared predicate fails the sharing test; adding a decision target fails the classification ratchet |
| **Status** | ✅ **FIXED 2026-08-01** — closes **A17-D2**. Code-only, **no migration** |
| **References** | `ChangeManagementService#requireConfiguredChangeAuthority` · `ChangeGovernanceHealthService.CONFIGURED_PREDICATE` · `ChangeAuthorityNotConfiguredException` · `GlobalExceptionHandler#handleChangeAuthorityNotConfigured` · `ChangeAuthorityController` · `CommitteeGateMapService` · `ChangeAuthorityPanel.tsx` · `ChangeAuthorityFailClosedTest` · `V310`/`V314` · [[F2-29]] · [[F2-62]] · [[F2-80]] |
| **Lesson** | Three. **(a) Making a defect visible is not fixing it, and a report is the easiest thing to mistake for a control** — F-4's remediation was a WARN plus a health endpoint, both accurate, and the fail-open survived them by a week because nothing was wired to refuse. **(b) A measurement is worth more than a size estimate:** this sat last in the queue at M→L on the premise that its config surface did not exist, when the surface had shipped in V310 seven days before that premise was written. *Re-measure the prerequisite before you inherit the estimate.* **(c) Fail-closed has a direction.** The naive reading — refuse every CAB-dependent transition — would have blocked *rejections*, protecting nothing and trapping changes somebody tried to kill |


## F2-88 · A duplicate committee code is a 500

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-88 *(found by A17-D2's own probe, 2026-08-01 — a re-run collided with its own leftover row)* |
| **Category** | Error contract — unique-constraint violation surfacing as an outage |
| **Severity** | **P3 Medium.** No data loss and no authorisation impact; the caller cannot tell a name clash from a broken server |
| **Current State** | `POST /api/admin/committees` with a `code` that already exists in the tenant → **`500 Internal Server Error`**. Reproduced deliberately: the same payload twice, 201 then 500 |
| **Problem** | The unique index on `admin.committees(organisation_id, code)` is enforced at flush, so the failure arrives as a `DataIntegrityViolationException`/`TransactionSystemException` rather than a validated 409 naming `code`. In **prod**, SEC-CFG-001 strips the root message, so the administrator gets a bare 500 plus a correlation id while chartering a committee |
| **Root Cause** | The same family as [[F2-84]] and [[F2-30]]: a constraint the code relies on but does not check, so the DB reports it in a form the error contract does not model. No pre-flight `findByOrganisationIdAndCode` exists on the create path, though **the repository method does** |
| **Business Impact** | Small but badly placed: it is on the first step of tenant onboarding for change governance — the step [[F2-87]]'s refusal routes an administrator to. "Constitute a committee" answering 500 turns a guided onboarding moment back into a support ticket |
| **Recommendation** | Pre-flight the code in `CommitteeService.createCommittee` and throw a 409 (or 400 naming `code`), and add a `DataIntegrityViolationException` handler as the backstop so the *next* unique index does not repeat this |
| **Effort** | **S** |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03.** Both halves the Recommendation asked for. **(1) Pre-flight** — `CommitteeService.createCommittee` now calls the `findByOrganisationIdAndCode` that already existed and was never called, throwing a new `DuplicateResourceException` → **409** with `field: "code"`, `conflictKind: "UNIQUE"` and an `errors` map in the shape the FE's `problemMessage()` already folds into a toast. ⚠ Not `BusinessRuleException`, which maps to **403** — that would tell an administrator they lack permission when the name is merely taken. **(2) Backstop** — a `DataIntegrityViolationException` handler maps the whole family by SQL Server error number: 2627/2601 → 409 `UNIQUE`, 547 → 409 `REFERENCE`, 515 → 400 `REQUIRED_FIELD`, anything else → 409 `INTEGRITY` (the default arm is the point — an unmapped code must not fall back to the 500 this finding was about). Measured blast radius: **264 unique non-PK indexes, 124 UNIQUE constraints, 181 foreign keys**, each previously a 500 of this exact shape. The response carries the constraint *identifier* and never the driver's message, which contains the schema **and the duplicate values** — tenant data, and what prod's SEC-CFG-001 strips root messages for. Suite **2,898/0/0**; live **12/12** (201 → 409 naming `code`, and reads/creates unaffected). ⚠ *Note on coverage: the backstop has no HTTP proof on this endpoint any more, precisely because the pre-flight now catches it first — the dev collation is case-insensitive, so even a case-variant duplicate is pre-flighted. Its coverage is `DataIntegrityErrorContractTest` (6 tests across the five error classes) plus the 264 indexes it newly protects* |
| **References** | `CommitteeController#createCommittee` · `CommitteeRepository#findByOrganisationIdAndCode` (the check that exists and is not called) · `GlobalExceptionHandler` · [[F2-84]] · [[F2-87]] |
| **Lesson** | **A probe that is idempotent enough to re-run will find the defects that only a second run reaches.** This was invisible on the first pass and mistaken for a regression on the second — the tell was that the 500 came from an endpoint the slice never touched |
| **➕ Reproduced again 2026-08-02** | W1-5's golden path hit this on its **second consecutive run**, on a code the first run had created. Not an edge case — it is what the second run of anything looks like, which raises the practical severity even though the technical one is unchanged. The IT works around it by purging its own prior artefacts before the class, so this remains open and undisguised |


## F2-89 · A committee motion cannot be raised undecided

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-89 *(found by W1-5's golden-path integration test, 2026-08-02)* |
| **Category** | Data model — a governance state that does not exist |
| **Severity** | **P2 High** for S7, **P3** today, because the only mode anyone has used is "record a decision already taken" |
| **Current State** | `admin.committee_resolutions.outcome` is **`NOT NULL` with no default**, and `CK_resolution_outcome` permits exactly `APPROVED \| REJECTED \| DEFERRED \| WITHDRAWN` — four **decided** states and nothing for *raised, awaiting the vote*. Omitting the field is `500 "An unexpected database error occurred."` Measured: **all 20 pre-existing resolutions are `APPROVED`**, tallies seeded as scalars |
| **Problem** | To decide a motion by in-platform voting you must first record a decision for it, then hold the vote that determines that decision. The contradiction is explicit in the codebase: F2-40's guard tells the caller *"to decide a motion by in-platform voting, create the resolution with no tally"* — possible for the tally, **impossible for the outcome**. `DEFERRED` is the least-wrong placeholder and still wrong: a committee actively deferring is a real governance act, and this borrows its label for "not yet asked" |
| **➕ The sharper half** | `CommitteeResolutionService.create` publishes an F8 domain event named **`COMMITTEE_RESOLUTION_` + outcome** at creation. So raising a motion for a vote broadcasts `COMMITTEE_RESOLUTION_DEFERRED` to downstream automation before anyone has voted — and a secretary who picked `APPROVED` as the placeholder would broadcast `COMMITTEE_RESOLUTION_APPROVED`, which that method's own comment says drives "regulator notification, board notification, risk-register update for accepted risks, policy publication for approved policies". A decision event fired by the act of *asking* the question |
| **Root Cause** | The resolution table was designed as a **minute-book record** — a row describing a decision that has already happened — and `CH-14`/Track C later added per-member voting on top without giving the record a pre-decision state. The same "designed for one mode, extended into a second" shape as F2-26's votes ([[F2-26]]) and F2-40's tallies ([[F2-40]]) |
| **Business Impact** | The agenda → motion → vote → decision cycle **S7** must build has no state for a live motion, so a CAB queue cannot show what is currently before the committee without misrepresenting it as decided |
| **Recommendation** | Add a pre-decision state (`PROPOSED`/`OPEN`) to `CK_resolution_outcome` **and** make the creation event carry it, so no downstream rule fires on a placeholder. Do it inside S7, whose row already requires the platform to *"state which decision mode is authoritative"* — a question that cannot be answered while only one of the two modes has a representable starting state |
| **Effort** | **S** (migration + vocabulary + the F8 event name), but it is a **decision** first |
| **⚠ Worse than the recommendation assumed** | Re-measured 2026-08-03 before fixing: **every placeholder a caller could pick fires a rule that is ENABLED in this database.** `APPROVED` → `AR-146` (regulator disclosure clock, EMAIL+SMS+INBOX, 24 h SLA) **and** `AR-147` ("file in the board-resolutions register within 48 h"); `DEFERRED` → `AR-152` ("re-table at the next meeting"). `AR-152` shows **`fire_count = 38`** while **zero** resolutions are `DEFERRED` — every fire was on a transient placeholder. The platform's own reference walkthrough is the proof: `CommitteeGoldenPathIT` posted `DEFERRED` with the comment *"there is no undecided state"*, so the canonical demonstration of correct committee governance emitted a false deferral on every run. And **the only UI that creates a resolution defaulted the field to `APPROVED`** (`CommitteesPage.resolutionInitial`) |
| **Fix** | **V349** adds `PROPOSED` to `CK_resolution_outcome` — deliberately *not* a decision, so `CommitteeDecisionAuthority.isDecided()` is a vocabulary test rather than a list of exceptions. `create()` defaults a blank outcome to `PROPOSED`, validates against the closed vocabulary (a bad value is now a **400** naming `PROPOSED`, not a 500), and publishes **`COMMITTEE_MOTION_RAISED`** for an undecided motion — a name no seeded rule listens to, which is correct: nothing has been decided, so nobody should be paged. The tally mapping is now a pure static function so *"no votes cast"* → `PROPOSED` is stated and tested rather than an unreachable branch; *"votes recorded but none FOR or AGAINST"* stays `DEFERRED`, which is a real outcome and the case `AR-152` is genuinely for. FE: `PROPOSED` is first in the picker, is the form default, and is bilingual (`مطروح`) |
| **The decision S7's row demanded** | Stated in `CommitteeDecisionAuthority`, and it is a **measurement**: **meeting-bound minutes is authoritative for every change type, because it is the only representable mode** — `meeting_id` is `NOT NULL`, so an asynchronous electronic vote has no representable form. ⚠ `assertDecisionApproves`'s `if (res.getMeetingId() != null)` is a **dead branch** that reads like a documented two-mode exemption; the permissive side is the unreachable one, so anyone making `meeting_id` nullable to add async voting would silently ship a decision path with no minutes gate. Now commented as such at the site |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03** (**V349**, with [[F2-100]], which was found while measuring this one). Live **23/23**, two real principals, no `PLATFORM_ADMIN`: a POST with no `outcome` returns `PROPOSED` with nothing announced and **AR-146/147/152 all unmoved**; `outcome:"CARRIED"` is a 400 naming `PROPOSED`. `CommitteeGoldenPathIT#raiseMotion` no longer sends an outcome and asserts `PROPOSED` through the real HTTP stack. Mutation-proven in both languages and both directions |
| **References** | `CommitteeDecisionAuthority` · `CommitteeResolutionService#create` · `#outcomeFor` · `V349` · `CommitteeMotionLifecycleTest` · `resolutionOutcomeVocabulary.test.ts` · `CommitteeGoldenPathIT#raiseMotion` · [[F2-100]] · [[F2-40]] · [[F2-18]] |
| **Lesson** | **A record with no undecided state can only ever describe the past.** The voting engine, the recusal rules and the quorum arithmetic were all built correctly on top of a table that has nowhere to put the question being asked. ⚠ And the half worth carrying: **the cost of a missing state is paid by whatever value gets borrowed to stand in for it.** `DEFERRED` was not a harmless placeholder — it was wired to a live rule, so 38 times the platform asked a secretary to re-table a motion that had never been tabled |


## F2-90 · Approving committee minutes wrote nothing to the audit ledger

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-90 *(found by W1-5's golden-path integration test on its final step, 2026-08-02)* |
| **Category** | Assurance / audit ledger completeness |
| **Severity** | **P2 High.** No authorisation impact — every gate held — but the platform's tamper-evident record omitted the act it is most needed for |
| **Current State** | Measured across the whole of `platform.audit_log`: **`MEETING_ATTENDANCE` was the only meeting action ever written**, and `audit.log` appeared exactly **once** in the whole of `CommitteeService`. Starting a meeting, completing it (which records the minutes text), **approving the minutes**, and cancelling it all wrote nothing |
| **Problem** | Minutes approval is the act that makes a committee decision binding. Four gates guard it — `COMPLETED`, quorate, quorum `COMPUTED` not asserted, and F2-39's separation from whoever recorded the attendance — and Track D asserts `CAB_APPROVED` on the result. An auditor reading the hash-chained ledger ([[F2-31]]) could see the **attendance sheet being replaced** and not the approval resting on it |
| **Why the e-signature is not a substitute** | It is a different subsystem behind a different permission (`SIGNATURE:READ` vs `AUDIT_LOG:READ`), it records a *signature* rather than an *event*, and it carries no actor role, correlation id or before/after status. "Signed" and "recorded in the ledger" are two claims, and the platform was making one while the SAMA/NCA evidence story rests on both |
| **Root Cause** | `recordAttendance` was written during F2-28 with an audit line because *that slice was about attendance provenance*. The surrounding lifecycle predates it and was never revisited. **No unit test could catch it:** 32 tests in `CommitteeQuorumMinutesTest` mock `AuditLogService` and none asserts a call on the approval path — the [[F2-18]] blind spot exactly, and it was found on the golden path's last step |
| **Business Impact** | A committee decision pack assembled from the audit ledger would show attendance edits and no approvals |
| **Status** | ✅ **DONE 2026-08-02** (no migration). `MEETING_START`, `MEETING_COMPLETE`, `MINUTES_APPROVE` and `MEETING_CANCEL` now written; `MINUTES_APPROVE` carries the approver, the persisted quorum (`present/denominator`, `required`, `quorumSource`) and the minutes SHA-256, so the ledger row states the preconditions rather than only the outcome. **Mutation-proven:** disabling the `MINUTES_APPROVE` line fails `CommitteeGoldenPathIT` with the expected/actual action-list diff |
| **References** | `CommitteeService#approveMinutes` · `CommitteeGoldenPathIT` step 14 · [[F2-31]] · [[F2-39]] · [[F2-18]] |
| **Lesson** | **The golden-path assertion was almost written as "the ledger is not empty", and that would have passed.** The meeting *did* have audit rows — for the sheet, not the sign-off. Asserting the named actions is what turned a green tick into a finding |


## F2-91 · The committee-type vocabulary was stated three times and wrong twice

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-91 *(found by W1-5's golden-path integration test on its FIRST call, 2026-08-02)* |
| **Category** | Boundary validation / vocabulary drift |
| **Severity** | **P2 High** — one half is a 500 on a well-formed request, the other silently refuses legal values |
| **Current State** | Three definitions: `CK_com_type` **22** values (`V40`, extended by `V154`); `BoardPackDto.audience`'s `@Pattern` **17**; `CommitteeCreateRequest.committeeType` **none** — only `@NotBlank @Size(max = 30)` |
| **Problem (a)** | The database CHECK was the *only* enforcement on committee creation, so an off-vocabulary type arrived as a `ConstraintViolationException` and surfaced as **500 "An unexpected database error occurred."** The field that **sets** the vocabulary was unvalidated while the field that merely **references** it was validated and ratcheted |
| **Problem (b)** | The board-pack pattern was missing `OTHER`, `ARCH_REVIEW`, `INVESTMENT`, `ALCO`, `AML`. **All five have been in `CK_com_type` since `V154` — 177 migrations before `V331` widened that pattern "to the real vocabulary"**, so the widening was five short on the day it shipped. A tenant with an ALCO or an Investment committee could not scope a pack to it, and the refusal said the value *"must be an `admin.committees.committee_type` value"* while being exactly that |
| **Root Cause** | ADR-017 D-11 recorded that this drift *"cannot recur unnoticed"* because `V331` throws when a **stored** audience fails to resolve. That guard is real and **points the wrong way**: it checks rows against the vocabulary, never the vocabulary's several definitions against each other. The direction that had actually drifted was the one nothing watched |
| **Status** | ✅ **DONE 2026-08-02** (no migration — the database was already right; the Java copies were wrong). `common.model.CommitteeTypes` is now the single statement; both DTOs validate against it and yield a 400 naming the field. **Two ratchets, deliberately split:** `CommitteeTypeVocabularyTest` ties every Java copy together in the ordinary suite, and `CommitteeGoldenPathIT` ties Java to `CK_com_type` **against a real database** — with a failure message naming which side is missing what and which failure it will cause. No unit test can make the second assertion, which is the clearest argument for W1-5 existing at all. **Mutation-proven in both directions** |
| **⚠ Not a defect, and worth stating** | The vocabulary has **no `CAB` or `ECAB`**, and that is ADR-017 D-2: *"the CAB is a role, not necessarily a standing committee."* The golden path's first draft tried to constitute a committee of type `CAB`; widening the vocabulary would have made the test pass **by reversing the decision that unblocked the whole change-governance epic**. `CommitteeTypeVocabularyTest#cabIsARoleNotACommitteeType` now pins the absence with the citation |
| **References** | `common.model.CommitteeTypes` · `CommitteeDtos.CommitteeCreateRequest` · `BoardPackDto#audience` · `V154` · `V331` · ADR-017 D-2, D-11 |
| **Lesson** | **A guard that checks data against a vocabulary is not a guard on the vocabulary.** D-11's claim was true and answered a different question — and the reflex fix for the 500 (add `CAB`) would have quietly overturned an owner-ratified decision, so *the first thing to check when a test fails on a vocabulary is whether the vocabulary is right* |


## F2-92 · The tenant write guard broke every insert in production, and nothing could log in

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-92 *(found by the 2026-08-02 prod deploy's own smoke test, not by a report)* |
| **Category** | Availability / a control that disabled the thing it protects |
| **Severity** | **P0.** Production could not authenticate a single user, and no entity could be inserted |
| **Current State** | With `grc.security.tenant-write-guard.enabled=true` — **hardcoded true in `application-prod.yml`, unset and therefore false in dev** — every insert into a table carrying an identity/`ROWVERSION` fails with `SQLServerException: A result set was generated for update.` Measured: `POST /api/auth/login` → **500** `"Unable to record refresh token — please retry."` because `platform.refresh_token` could not be written. Flip the flag off and the same request returns **200**. |
