| **Problem** | ~~It is the interceptor being **registered on the session factory** that breaks inserts~~ — ⚠ **refuted 2026-08-03, see Status: dev has run with it registered since 2026-07-26 and inserts fine.** What survives is the narrower half: the interceptor's own logic never ran, because `platform.audit_log` holds **zero `SECURITY_BLOCK` rows, ever** (re-confirmed in both databases). So a defence-in-depth backstop coincided with the platform going down without blocking anything — the *coincidence* is established, the *causation* was inferred and is now disproved |
| **How long it was live, and why nobody knew** | The most recent successful prod login before this was **2026-07-28** — five days. The interceptor is **inert in dev by flag**, and its own javadoc says so out loud: *"a dev-machine HTTP probe exercises the per-service stamp, **never this bean**."* `application-prod.yml`'s own comment adds the condition that was skipped: *"Activate only after the documented staging soak … watch the audit log for any `SECURITY_BLOCK` entries on legitimate flows before relying on it."* Nothing exercised it, so nothing caught it |
| **Ruled out, by measurement rather than reasoning** | Not caused by the 2026-08-02 deploy: `TenantWriteGuardInterceptor`, `TenantWriteGuardConfig`, `RefreshTokenStoreService`, `RefreshToken` and `AuthController` are **byte-identical** between the previous jar and this one (`md5` over the extracted `.class` files), and **none of V337–V344 touches `refresh_token`**. Not schema drift: prod and dev column types, nullability, defaults, identity and `ROWVERSION` are identical, on the **same SQL Server instance and build** (`15.0.2000.5`) with identical `compatibility_level` and every ANSI/isolation database option equal. Not a trigger: `sys.triggers` has exactly one trigger in the database and it is on `socops.forensics_custody_event` |
| **Root Cause** | Prime suspect is Hibernate 6's handling of the deprecated legacy `Interceptor#onSave(...)` hook: implementing it changes the insert strategy so the generated-value read-back is executed through `executeUpdate()`, and SQL Server answers a result set. **Not yet proven** — the proof needs the flag enabled in dev, which is precisely the reproduction nobody has ever run |
| **Business Impact** | The deployed platform was unusable — a 500 on the first screen — for anyone who tried between 07-28 and 08-02. Discovered by our own probe rather than by a customer, but only just |
| **Interim disposition** | ⚠ **Flag set to `false` in `D:\Deployment\tss-grc\app\external.properties`** with a long operator note giving this finding's id and the re-enable condition. Prod verified working afterwards: login 200, five authenticated reads 200, refresh-token rows written again. Per-service `setOrganisationId(TenantContext.getCurrentTenant())` stamps remain the **primary** tenant control; this bean is only the backstop, and a backstop that prevents all writes protects nothing |
| **Recommendation** | (1) **Enable the flag in dev** and reproduce — that single missing step is the finding's real root cause and it is one line of config. (2) Migrate the hook to the non-deprecated Hibernate 6 API (`onPersist`/an event listener) and re-verify inserts with generated values. (3) Add an integration test with the guard **on**, in the `it` profile W1-5 just created — `CommitteeGoldenPathIT` writes committees, meetings, votes and changes, so it is already the right harness. (4) Only then re-enable in prod |
| **Effort** | **S** to reproduce and probably S to fix; the test is the durable part |
| **Status** | ⬜ **Still open — P0, mitigated. ⚠ ROOT CAUSE CORRECTED 2026-08-03 — the one recorded here is refuted.** The fix plan's own first step (*enable the flag in dev and reproduce*) was carried out and **the defect does not reproduce.** (1) The flag has been `enabled: true` in `application-dev.yml` since `695c907c` — **2026-07-26, seven days before this finding was filed** — contradicting the premise that it was unset in dev. (2) The dev backend logs `[TenantWriteGuard] interceptor WIRED` on every boot and `platform.refresh_token` holds **2,075 rows, the newest written that morning**. (3) `TenantWriteGuardInterceptor` and `TenantWriteGuardConfig` are **byte-identical between the deployed commit `473da706` and HEAD**. (4) Both databases sit on the same SQL Server instance and match on schema, compatibility level 150, collation, snapshot options, the single trigger in either database (on an unrelated table), and `refresh_token`'s exact `IDENTITY`+`ROWVERSION` shape. Now **pinned by `TenantWriteGuardOnIT`** (4 tests, `it` profile, 21 s): the real application boots with the flag ON, logs in, writes the refresh-token row, completes an identity insert, and still refuses a cross-tenant one. **The profile is now eliminated too (2026-08-03, second pass).** Booted locally on the **`prod` profile** with the guard explicitly ON, SSL and Flyway off, against the dev database: `[TenantWriteGuard] interceptor WIRED`, `POST /api/auth/login` → **200**, and `platform.refresh_token` went **2100 → 2101**. So the code, the schema, the database options *and the profile* all fail to reproduce it. **What is left is the deployed runtime itself** — Flyway-at-startup (the one variable deliberately excluded, since running it against dev would migrate a Python-bootstrapped schema), the named-instance JDBC URL (`localhost\SQLEXPRESS` vs `localhost:1433`), the WinSW service account, and the rest of `external.properties`. Narrowing further needs the flag switched back on in production under supervision, which is an operator decision and not a test's. ⚠ *Two incidental notes for whoever does it: the prod profile refuses to boot without an SSL keystore (`server.ssl.*`, supplied by `external.properties` on the server) and pins **both** `server.port` 8090 and the management port — a local prod-profile boot must override both or it collides with the live service.* Flag stays `false` in prod meanwhile, so **production still has no Hibernate-level cross-tenant backstop**; the per-service `setOrganisationId` stamps remain the only control |
| **References** | `TenantWriteGuardInterceptor#onSave`/`#decideInsert` · `TenantWriteGuardConfig` · `application-prod.yml` `grc.security.tenant-write-guard.enabled` · `RefreshTokenStoreService#recordIssuance` · `docs/security/MASS_ASSIGNMENT_DEFENSE.md` · [[F2-18]] |
| **Lesson (2026-08-03)** | **A root cause that was never reproduced is a hypothesis wearing a conclusion's clothes — and this one was load-bearing.** Believed, it says a P0 control is inherently unsafe and must stay off forever. The finding even *named* the missing step — "enable the flag in DEV and reproduce — that missing step **is** the root cause" — and then wrote a causal sentence as though the step had been taken. Worse, the environment claim it rested on ("unset in dev") was **already false in the repository**, one `git log` away, when it was written. **If a fix plan says "reproduce it first", the causal sentence waits.** |
| **Lesson** | **A control that is off in dev and on in prod is not tested — it is deployed and hoped for.** This is the same shape as F2-62's dead flag and A17-D2's fail-open WARN, with the polarity reversed: instead of a control that did nothing, a control that did far too much. Both were invisible for the same reason — *the only environment that runs it is the one nobody probes.* Its javadoc named the gap ("never this bean") and its config named the missing precondition ("only after the documented staging soak"); **two accurate warnings, written by us, consumed by nobody** |


## F2-93 · The change record says when the CAB decided and never who

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-93 *(found while building A17-D3, because the control D-3 specifies could not be enforced without it)* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` |
| **Severity** | **P2** on its own; **blocking** for A17-D3 |
| **Current State** | `change_mgmt.change_requests` carried `cab_decision_at` and `cab_decision_notes` and **no column naming the decider**. The author of a CAB approval, a CAB rejection or an emergency ECAB approval existed only in `platform.audit_log` |
| **Problem** | Two consequences, one obvious and one not. Obvious: an examiner asking *"who authorised this emergency change?"* is answered from a log rather than from the record — and the log is a different retention class from the register. Not obvious: **ADR-017 D-3's segregation-of-duties test had nothing to compare against.** Retrospective ratification exists to supply the independent scrutiny the emergency path skipped; if the emergency approver may also ratify, the control certifies its own author's decision *and* the evidence pack asserts that a second party reviewed it. That is [[F2-29]] one lifecycle step later |
| **Root Cause** | The decision fields were added as *timestamp + free text*. Provenance was treated as an audit concern, and the audit trail genuinely does hold it — which is exactly why nobody noticed the register did not |
| **Fix** | **V345** adds `cab_decision_by_id` / `cab_decision_by_name`, stamped from the authenticated principal on all three CAB-decision transitions — never from a request body (the [[F2-24]] rule). **Not back-filled from the audit ledger**: the ledger holds usernames, not the identity the SoD test compares, and inferring one from the other manufactures provenance for a decision nobody can now attest to. Legacy rows keep NULL, and `GRANDFATHERED` is what stops that NULL being read as "no conflict" |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed** — V345, `ChangeManagementService#stampDecisionAuthor`, live-probed (`cabDecisionByName=compliance`, `cabDecisionById=3`) |
| **Lesson** | **A control cannot be enforced against a fact the record does not hold.** The SoD rule was decided in ADR-017 nine days before anyone checked whether the comparison it requires was possible. Measuring the *inputs* a decision needs is part of measuring whether the decision is buildable — the amendment pass checked for ratification columns and missed that the approver was equally absent |


## F2-94 · 126 of 164 automation rules notify nobody — the engine reads `to_role`, the rules all write `roles`

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-94 *(found by A17-D3's live probe: the escalation fired, was audited, and reached no one)* |
| **Module** | `platform/automation` (F8 engine) — affects `change_mgmt`, `policy`, `bcm`, `privacy`, `risk`, `sust`, `socops` and every other module that seeded a rule |
| **Severity** | **P1.** The platform's entire notification/escalation fan-out is inert for 77% of its rules |
| **Current State** | `AutomationActionExecutor.handleNotify` resolves the recipient from `action_json.to_role` (ESCALATE) or `action_json.role` (NOTIFY) — **singular string keys**. Every seeded rule from V96 onward writes `"roles": ["CISO","CTO",…]` — **a plural array the executor never reads**. Measured 2026-08-02 in both databases: **dev 126 of 164 NOTIFY/ESCALATE rule codes (153 rows across 3 tenants); prod 125 of 161 (150 rows)**. Only **12** rules — `AR-06/09/10/12/13` and the seven `BCM-T*` — actually deliver |
| **Problem** | The rule fires, the engine records `RULE_FIRED`, `automation_rule_runs` gets a row, `fire_count` increments — and `handleNotify` returns `ESCALATE (no role configured — audit-only)` having sent nothing. Every downstream signal says the automation worked. Among the dead rules: `AR-97` HIGH-risk **emergency change approved → escalate CTO + CISO**, `AR-72` **expired policy → escalate owner**, `AR-73` HIGH-risk policy exception → CRO + General Counsel, `AR-75` attestation declined → manager + DPO, `CHG-TASK-OVERDUE`, `POL-REVIEW-DUE` |
| **How long it was live, and why nobody knew** | Since the seeds were written. **`platform.audit_log` already holds 278 rows in dev and 164 in prod whose message is `has no role configured`** — the engine has been reporting its own inertness continuously and the WARN was never read. Third instance of that exact shape in this programme after [[F2-87]]'s boot WARN and [[F2-92]]'s javadoc |
| **Root Cause** | A comment asserting an agreement nobody measured. The executor's own javadoc says the singular naming *"match[es] the conventional naming in the seed rules"*; measured, it matches **12 of 164** of them. The two halves were authored independently and the prose bridged them |
| **Fix** | **A17-D3 fixed only its own rule** (`CHG-RATIFY-BREACH` → `"to_role":"CISO"`, live-verified as `ESCALATE → CISO (1 delivered)` landing in `so_officer`'s inbox), plus a V345 self-check that THROWs if the key drifts back. The other 125 need their own slice. **The better fix is almost certainly in the executor** — accept `roles[]` as well as `role`/`to_role`: one code change instead of 150 data rows in two databases, it matches what the seed rules plainly intend (multi-role fan-out, which a singular key cannot express), and it stops the next rule authored from the V305 pattern breaking the same way. ⚠ **But switching it on begins delivering ~150 rules' worth of notifications that have never been sent**, to real inboxes, for events already in the backlog. That is an operational decision, not a refactor |
| **Effort** | **S** for the executor change; **S–M** for the notification-volume assessment that must precede it |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03** — `AutomationActionExecutor.RECIPIENT_KEYS` publishes a **closed** three-key vocabulary (`roles`, `role`, `to_role`) read by one resolver, and `AutomationRecipientKeyRatchetTest` checks **every migration** against that same constant, so the vocabulary cannot drift open again. `NotificationService.broadcastToRoles` fans out to all addressed roles with recipients de-duplicated, and returns a `RoleFanout` splitting delivered / unheld / **not-a-role**. V346 normalises the one row the code fix could not reach (AR-148) and catalogues `AUTOMATION:READ`. Suite **2,885/0/0**, ratchet mutation-proven, 19/20 live. **127 of 140 dead rule codes → 61**, and the 61 are now *reported* rather than silent |
| **References** | `AutomationActionExecutor#handleNotify` (the `to_role`/`role` reads) · `V96__automation_rules.sql` · `V305__change_policy_automation_seed.sql` · `V345__change_ratification_sla.sql` (the corrected shape + the self-check) · [[F2-52]] · [[F2-62]] |
| **Lesson** | **A fired rule is not a delivered notification, and every metric the engine keeps conflates them.** `fire_count`, `last_fired_at`, `automation_rule_runs` and the `RULE_FIRED` audit row all increment identically whether one person or nobody was notified — so the observable surface is indistinguishable in the two cases. This is [[F2-62]]'s dead control with better telemetry: the numbers went up. **When a subsystem's success metric is "we tried", the only proof of the control is the recipient.** A17-D3 only found it because its live probe asked *who received this*, not *did it fire* |


## F2-95 · The authorisation matrix demands roles on 33 of 36 rows and enforces none of them

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-95 *(found by re-measuring S6's premises before building it)* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` (CH-17's authorisation matrix) |
| **Severity** | **P1** |
| **Current State** | `change_mgmt.change_authorisation_matrix.required_roles` is **configured on 33 of 36 rows in dev and prod alike** — `CHANGE_MANAGER`, `CHANGE_MANAGER,CISO`, `CHANGE_MANAGER,CISO,CTO`. It is read in exactly one place: `resolveAuthorisation` does `out.put("requiredRoles", …)`. Its own javadoc concedes it — *"advisory"*. `satisfied` in the same payload is `approves >= required`, **counting votes and ignoring roles entirely** |
| **Problem** | The tenant has configured "a CRITICAL change requires the CISO", the screen displays it beside a `satisfied` flag, and the change approves with no CISO anywhere near it. The API reports a requirement it does not apply, which is worse than not having the field: a reviewer reading `requiredRoles: CISO` next to `satisfied: true` concludes a CISO signed off |
| **Root Cause, and it is the interesting part** | **There is no vocabulary in which the check could be made.** Three disconnected role vocabularies sit on this one decision path: (1) `required_roles` uses **platform role names** (`CISO`); (2) `committee_member_terms.role` uses **governance seat titles** (`Chair`, `Member`, `Secretary`, `Independent Director`); (3) `change_approvals.approver_role` is **free text the voter types** (`CAB Chair`, `Member — Cybersecurity`, `Security`, `Legal`). **No value from (1) appears in (2) or (3)** — so enforcing it literally would refuse almost every CAB approval. ⚠ *Measured 2026-08-03 rather than asserted: **9 of 11** decided changes in dev and **8 of 10** in prod, and the reason is sharper than the vocabulary mismatch — `CHANGE_MANAGER` is required on all 33 rows and **has no active holder in either database**. The seed is internally contradictory too: the approver named "Chief Information Security Officer", capacity "Member — Cybersecurity", holds the platform role `RISK_MANAGER`.* And `CTO`, named on the CRITICAL rows, **is not a row in `iam.roles` at all** ([[F2-52]]'s ghost-role shape, a fourth instance) |
| **The enforceable path — no fourth vocabulary needed** | [[F2-26]] linked committee seats to platform users, so `member_user_id → iam.user_roles` answers "was a CISO present?" in the platform's own authorisation vocabulary. Measured: **6 of 29 ACTIVE voting seats are linked**; `CISO` **is** reachable through a linked seat, `CHANGE_MANAGER` is not held by any linked seat, and `CTO` does not exist |
| **Therefore the fix must distinguish two failures** | *Required role absent* → 409, a decision failure. *Required role unresolvable* (a ghost role, or one no linked seat could ever hold) → a **configuration fault** surfaced in `governance-health`, not a 409. Today every required role except `CISO` is unresolvable, so the fault would fire loudly and block nothing; the moment a tenant links seats it starts biting. Switching enforcement on without that split would refuse every CAB approval on day one — [[F2-92]]'s mistake in a different costume |
| **Effort** | ~~**M**~~ — the split, the resolution and the payload shipped as S6b-i; the enforcement gate is **S** once the two decisions are made |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03 — both halves (S6b-i reported, S6b-ii enforced).** `requiredRoleStates` gives each configured role one of **SATISFIED / MISSING / NO_ACTIVE_HOLDER / NOT_A_ROLE**, resolved through `approver_id → iam.user_roles`; `satisfied` is now the conjunction and cannot read true over an unmet or unverifiable requirement. **The gate refuses `CAB_APPROVED` with a 409 when — and only when — a role is `MISSING`** (checkable here, held by no approver). ⚠ **Owner decisions taken 2026-08-03:** (a) `CTO` **stripped**, not created — **V347** — because it is *our* seed on 6 identical rows in both databases, and creating a role with no persona, no grants and no holder would fabricate an access model (F2-62's dead control); V347 also THROWs if *any* required role is absent from `iam.roles`, which is the invariant that makes the gate expressible. (b) `NO_ACTIVE_HOLDER` / `NOT_A_ROLE` **never block** — a literal gate would have refused **9 of 11** decided changes in dev and **8 of 10** in prod, because `CHANGE_MANAGER` is required on all 33 rows and no active user in either database holds it; refusing there leaves the approver no action that could succeed, which is [[F2-92]]'s shape. `CHANGE_MANAGER` stays on the rows — deleting a requirement the tenant configured would quietly lower a control; the remedy is to grant it. **Not enforced at `EMERGENCY_APPROVED`, deliberately**: an emergency change is authorised fast and *ratified* afterwards (A17-D3), so the after-the-fact check belongs on the ratification path — filed rather than smuggled in. Suite **2,903/0/0**; live **14/14** in both directions. ⚠ *Probe note: F2-96's committee gate fires first, so both of org 1's gate committees must be seated for the role gate to be reachable at all — then unseated, because the empty roster is F2-96's recorded defect* |
| **References** | `ChangeAuthorisationMatrix#requiredRoles` (the "advisory" javadoc) · `ChangeManagementService#resolveAuthorisation` · `CommitteeMemberTerm#memberUserId` · [[F2-26]] · [[F2-52]] · [[F2-62]] |
| **Lesson** | **A field that three subsystems name differently cannot be enforced, and "advisory" is what a developer writes when they discover that mid-implementation.** The word in the javadoc *was* the finding, and nobody followed it up for as long as the column has existed. When a config field's javadoc says *advisory*, *best-effort* or *informational*, treat it as an unfiled defect and check whether the reason is that the check is impossible |


## F2-96 · Both change-authority gates point at committees that can never be quorate — and health said fine

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-96 *(found by re-measuring S6's premises; the same pass that found [[F2-95]])* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` / `admin.committee` |
| **Severity** | **P1** |
| **Current State** | Measured 2026-08-02, **identically in dev and production**: org 1's `CAB` gate maps to committee 13 *IT Steering Committee* and its `ECAB` gate to committee 12 *Executive Committee*. Both are `ACTIVE` and correctly mapped — and **both have zero ACTIVE voting seats**. `GET /api/v1/changes/governance-health` answered **`healthy: true`** |
| **Problem** | A committee with no voting seat can never be quorate: `computeQuorum` sets `quorumMet = 0` whenever `totalVoting == 0` (correctly, since F2-28). So every change referred to the CAB or ECAB was **undecidable**, and every *non-referred* CAB-gated change was being recorded as **CAB approved in the name of a body with no members** — while the governance screen reported the tenant healthy. A17-D2's predicate asked *"is a committee mapped?"*; the screen it feeds claims *"changes routed to this authority can be authorised"* |
| **Root Cause** | The same shape as [[F2-87]] and [[F2-62]]: **the report measured the mapping, not the capability.** A17-D2 correctly added existence and `DISSOLVED` checks — matching what `CommitteeReferralService.refer` enforces — and stopped one clause short of the property that actually decides whether a decision can happen |
| **Fix** | The roster clause is added to `CONFIGURED_PREDICATE` — the **single** definition shared by the report (`GAP_SQL`) and the enforcement path (`CONFIGURED_ONE_SQL` → `requireConfiguredChangeAuthority`) — so the report became honest and the gate began failing closed **in the same edit**. Two predicates would have let them drift apart again, which is the defect. A third fault kind `ROSTER_EMPTY` is returned **before** `COMMITTEE_UNAVAILABLE` (every empty committee also satisfies "a row exists", so the broad test would swallow the narrow one) and its remedy names *appointing members*, saying explicitly that re-mapping the gate is **not** the fix — the mapping is correct and the body is empty |
| **⚠ Deliberate behavioural consequence** | A tenant whose mapped committee has an empty roster now has CAB/ECAB approvals **refused** until members are appointed. That is ADR-017 D-2's accepted trade — fail closed, with a guided onboarding moment naming the screen. **It applies to the deployed demo tenants**: org 1's two gates are both empty in production today, so after the next prod deploy CAB/ECAB approvals there will be refused until voting members are appointed to committees 12 and 13 |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed** — one clause, three faults, `ChangeAuthorityRosterCapabilityTest` (5 tests, including one asserting that exactly **one** definition of "configured" exists), 10/10 live in **both** directions: empty → `healthy:false` + 409 `reason=ROSTER_EMPTY`; seat both gates → `healthy:true` + the approval proceeds; seats then removed so dev is left as found |
| **References** | `ChangeGovernanceHealthService#CONFIGURED_PREDICATE` · `ChangeAuthorityNotConfiguredException#ROSTER_EMPTY` · `CommitteeService#computeQuorum` · [[F2-28]] · [[F2-87]] |
| **Lesson** | **Fail-closed is not one decision, it is a chain, and it is only as strong as the last property nobody thought to check.** A17-D2 asked "is there a committee?"; this asks "can it decide?"; the next question is [[F2-95]]'s "were the right people in the room?" Each looked complete when it shipped. The way to find the next link is to take what the *report* claims and test that claim rather than the query behind it — `healthy: true` was falsifiable in one SQL statement for the eight days since A17-D2 shipped |


## F2-97 · One automation rule addresses a person the engine can only address as a role

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-97 *(surfaced by [[F2-94]]'s ratchet — it is the single rule the closed vocabulary cannot excuse)* |
| **Module** | `platform/automation` (F8) / `admin.committee` action items |
| **Severity** | **P3** |
| **Current State** | `AR-149` — trigger `COMMITTEE_ACTION_CREATED`, action `{"type":"NOTIFY","channels":[…],"template":"CRITICAL_ACTION_ASSIGNED","slaMinutes":60}` — names **no audience at all**, under any key. It is the only NOTIFY/ESCALATE rule in the platform of which that is true |
| **Problem** | The rule's intended recipient is the *assignee of the committee action*, a **per-user** address. `NOTIFY` can only address a **role**, so the rule as written can never deliver: the executor reports `NOTIFY (no audience addressed — audit-only)` and a critical action assignment reaches its owner through no channel |
| **Why it is filed rather than fixed** | The two ways to close it are both wrong on their own. Giving it a role (`COMMITTEE_SECRETARY`, say) fabricates intent — the template says *assigned*, and a secretary is not the assignee. Teaching `NOTIFY` to address a user requires an addressing mode the action vocabulary does not have, which is a design decision about the F8 engine, not a data repair. `NotificationService#notifyUser` already exists for exactly this shape, so the engine work is small — but it is engine work |
| **Effort** | **S** once the addressing mode is decided: a `"user_field"` (or similar) key resolved against the event payload, then `notifyUser` |
| **Status** | ⬜ **Open — P3.** Allow-listed by name in `AutomationRecipientKeyRatchetTest.AUDIENCE_ABSENT_BY_DESIGN`, which asserts the entry still corresponds to a rule that is still audience-less and still cites a finding — so the allowlist cannot outlive what it excuses |
| **References** | `V158__committee_automation_rules.sql` (AR-149) · `AutomationActionExecutor#RECIPIENT_KEYS` · `NotificationService#notifyUser` · [[F2-94]] |
| **Lesson** | **An allowlist entry is a filed defect or it is a silencer.** The ratchet needed one exception; making the entry carry a finding id, and testing that the excused rule is still broken, is what keeps the difference visible. The alternative — inventing a role so the count reached zero — would have made the report green by making the platform wrong |


## F2-98 · The "Demo mode" badge tracks the session, not the substitution

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-98 *(found by S5c while splitting `ChangesPage`; the file being restructured was one of the four)* |
| **Module** | frontend — `pages/change`, `pages/ethics`, `pages/risk`, `pages/reporting` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a signed-in user cannot distinguish fabricated records from their own |
| **Current State** | Four pages hand-roll the demo-data fallback instead of using `useApiResource`. All four call their `applyDemo*()` from **three** places — not signed in, the register came back **empty**, and the API **threw** — and all four disclose it with `{demoMode && <Badge>Demo mode</Badge>}` where `const demoMode = !isAuthenticated()`, a value computed once at render |
| **Problem** | `!isAuthenticated()` is **false** for exactly the two cases a real tenant can hit. A fresh tenant with no changes yet, or any user while the backend is down, is shown a full register of invented records **with no badge**. So the disclosure fires for the case that was already obvious (you are not logged in) and stays silent for the two that are not. Measured live: an empty `200` and a network failure both put **10 fabricated change requests** on screen; the export menu stayed enabled and, when disabled, said *"Sign in to export"* to a user who was signed in — and the file it would have produced is the real, empty register, so screen and export disagree |
| **Why the existing guard missed it** | `demoFallbackContract.test.ts` already asserts *"every page consuming `useApiList`/`useApiResource` surfaces `isDemo`"* — 10 of 10 hook consumers pass. Its trigger is the **hook**. These four pages reimplement the behaviour, so they were never in scope. A guard scoped to a mechanism tolerates every reimplementation of it; the same distinction `BARE_EXPORT_CEILING` → `everyExportHandlerAlsoDemandsARead` turned on |
| **Fix** | Make the flag **state the substitution sets**, exactly as the hook does (`isDemo` starts at `!isAuthenticated()` and is then set by each substitution path), and raise it **inside the applier** so the two cannot drift apart again. ⚠ *"Three lines per page" was my estimate and it was wrong for three of the four:* `EthicsPage` and `RiskRegisterPage` substitute in **child tabs** that receive `demoMode` as a prop, and a parent's single header badge cannot speak for them — the cases list can be the tenant's own while the AML alerts beside it are invented. Each child now discloses locally through a shared `DemoDataNotice`. Building that component also closed a second defect the four hand-rolled sites had already drifted into: **two of them rendered a hard-coded English sentence** in an Arabic-primary product, and both ended *"Sign in for live …"* — precisely the wrong advice for the signed-in reader who needs the notice |
| **Effort** | **S** — the remaining three pages are the same three lines each |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03 — all four pages, no allow-list left.** Live-verified **9/9**: `change` 3/3 (empty `200` · network failure · real rows clear it) in **both languages**, `reporting` badge absent→present, `ethics` cases badge absent→present, `ethics` AML and KYC notices absent on real data and present on substituted, `risk` KRI and appetite the same. Zero console errors throughout. ⚠ **The guard needed rewriting mid-slice and that is the keeper.** Its first draft searched for `set(Demo|IsDemo)…(true)` anywhere in the file — **name**-based, and wrong twice: it reported the correctly-fixed `RiskRegisterPage` as broken (its state is called `showingDemo`) and it passed `EthicsPage` on the strength of its *parent's* setter while both child tabs were still silent. It now brace-matches each `apply*()` body that is actually on a substitution path and requires a `set…(true)` **inside it**, with an anti-vacuity floor — behavioural, not nominal |
| **References** | `demoFallbackContract.test.ts` (widened guard + allow-list) · `lib/useApiResource.ts` (the correct model) · `ExportMenu.disabledReason` · [[F2-42]] (the same "fabricated where the server has an answer" shape) · W2-1b (which pinned the *disclosed* contract deliberately) |
| **Lesson** | **A disclosure bound to the wrong condition is worse than none — it reads as a considered decision.** W2-1b was right that demo substitution is designed, not a defect, and right to pin it rather than delete it; what it could not see is that the flag it pinned was `isDemo` on the hook, while four pages carried a same-named `demoMode` meaning something else. My own first scan called `ChangesPage` *disclosed* because the file **contains** `demoMode` — the presence of a disclosure token is not disclosure of the right event |


## F2-99 · A misspelt JSON key erased a committee's attendance sheet, and answered 200

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-99 *(found by S6c's own live probe — the probe had the bug, and the platform's response to it was the finding)* |
| **Module** | `admin/committee` — `POST /api/admin/committees/meetings/{id}/attendance` |
| **Severity** | **P3** — fails closed and is fully audited, but destroys governance evidence on a typo |
| **Current State** | The endpoint **replaces the whole sheet**: it deletes every attendance row and rebuilds from `req.marks()`. The platform sets `spring.jackson.deserialization.fail-on-unknown-properties: false` — a deliberate forward-compatibility choice — so my probe's `{"attendees": [...]}` deserialised to `marks == null`, the service's `req.marks() == null ? List.of() : …` read that as "nobody attended", and the call answered **200** having deleted a 4-row sheet and recomputed quorum to not-met |
| **Problem** | Attendance is the *only* origin of quorum since F2-28/V319, and quorum is the precondition for approving minutes, which is the precondition for authorising a change or publishing a policy. So a single mistyped key at the top of that chain silently discards the record the whole chain rests on. It took three probe runs to notice, and only because every downstream assertion read `present=0/2` |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a security hole and not silent in the ledger: quorum goes to **not met**, so it fails closed, and `MEETING_ATTENDANCE` records *"Attendance sheet replaced · 4 row(s) → 0"* exactly as F2-28's register item 6 requires. The audit trail was right; the API was too forgiving |
| **Fix** | `@NotNull` on `AttendanceRequest.marks` — **deliberately not `@NotEmpty`**. An empty list is a legitimate assertion ("nobody attended"); per `AttendanceMark`'s own javadoc an unmarked voting seat is *absent* and still counts in the denominator. The distinction that matters is **absent field** (a mistake — now a 400 naming `marks`) versus **empty list** (something the caller meant). The global Jackson setting is left alone: flipping `fail-on-unknown-properties` platform-wide would change every endpoint's contract to fix one of them |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03** (no migration). Live 5/5: a good sheet records 4 present · the misspelt key answers **400** with `{"marks": "Field is required"}` · **the existing sheet survives the rejected request** · `[]` still returns 200 with 0 present |
| **References** | `CommitteeDtos.AttendanceRequest` · `CommitteeService#recordAttendance` · `application.yml:108` · [[F2-28]] (which made attendance the sole origin of quorum, and therefore made this matter) |
| **Lesson** | **A replace-the-collection endpoint has to distinguish "you sent nothing" from "you sent an empty set".** With unknown properties ignored — a reasonable platform-wide choice — every such endpoint reads a typo as a deliberate wipe. Worth sweeping the other `List<…>`-replacing request bodies for the same shape; that is a separate slice, and it is filed rather than claimed. ⚠ And the meta-lesson: **my probe was wrong three times before I read the endpoint's own DTO.** `present=0/2` was the platform reporting the truth about a request I had malformed, and I spent two edits looking for the defect in my new code |


## F2-100 · A committee decision reached by voting is announced to nobody; raising the motion pages the secretary

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-100 *(found while measuring F2-89's blast radius for S7a — the two are the same publish site and the same decision)* |
| **Module** | `admin/committee` — `CommitteeResolutionService`, `CommitteeService#approveMinutes` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a statutory disclosure clock and a board-register filing instruction both fire on the wrong event, in both directions |
| **Current State** | The whole module had **exactly one** `publishEvent` for resolution outcomes, in `create()`. `recomputeTally()` — the method that turns counted votes into an outcome — published nothing, and `approveMinutes()` — the act that makes a decision binding — published nothing. So `COMMITTEE_RESOLUTION_<outcome>` fired **only** when a tally was typed in from paper minutes, never when a motion was decided in-platform |
| **Proven from the ledger, not argued** | Resolutions **69 and 70** were created 2026-08-03 09:56:40 and voted to `APPROVED` (2/0/0 and 1/0/0, `tally_source=COMPUTED`). `AR-147` (`COMMITTEE_RESOLUTION_APPROVED` → "file in the board-resolutions register within 48 h") has `last_fired_at` **2026-07-26 14:19:20** — a week earlier. It provably never fired for either. Meanwhile `AR-152` (`COMMITTEE_RESOLUTION_DEFERRED` → "re-table at the next meeting") shows `fire_count = 38` while **zero** resolutions in the database are `DEFERRED`: every one of those 38 fires was on [[F2-89]]'s transient placeholder |
| **Problem** | The behaviour was exactly inverted: **raising a motion notified the secretary to re-table it, and deciding the motion notified nobody.** The provenance-bearing path — [[F2-40]]'s `COMPUTED`, the one with per-member vote rows and recusals — is the path automation cannot see. `AR-146` exists to start a regulator disclosure clock ("so the disclosure clock starts visibly", CMA/SAMA/SDAIA); it cannot start on a decision it is never told about |
| **What it is NOT** | Not the cause of `AR-146`'s 0 fires. The three `regulator_notify = 1` resolutions are all `ASSERTED_LEGACY`, seeded directly on 2026-05-23 and never passed through `create()`, so no event could have fired for them. That is a seeding artefact and saying otherwise would overstate the finding |
| **Fix** | **One publish site**, `publishIfBinding`, guarded by a new `decision_published_at` column (**V349**), called from all three moments a decision can become binding: create, the deciding vote, and minutes approval. Whichever happens last publishes; the other two find it stamped. All three are needed because both orders occur in real data — **19 of 22** resolutions in dev sit on meetings that were already `MINUTES_APPROVED` when the resolution was recorded, so publishing only at minutes approval would have announced nothing for the dominant pattern, the mirror image of the bug. Never throws: an announcement failure must not roll back a recorded vote |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03** (**V349**, with [[F2-89]]). Live **23/23** with two real principals and no `PLATFORM_ADMIN`: a motion voted to APPROVED moved **AR-147 5 → 6** — the APPROVED rule firing for an in-platform decision for the first time — while **AR-152 stayed at 38**. A second vote did not re-announce it. Asserted end-to-end in `CommitteeGoldenPathIT` at a new declared step, because the ordering is invisible to a unit test: the votes were cast while the meeting was `IN_PROGRESS`, so minutes approval is the only moment the decision could become binding |
| **References** | `CommitteeResolutionService#publishIfBinding` · `#publishBindingDecisionsForMeeting` · `CommitteeDecisionAuthority` · `V349` · `V158` (AR-146/147/152) · [[F2-89]] · [[F2-40]] · [[F2-90]] |
| **Lesson** | **An event published at creation attests to nothing that has happened yet.** The defect and its inverse were one line apart, and the measurement that separated them was a timestamp comparison: `last_fired_at` earlier than the row it should have fired for. ⚠ And a live probe of an async engine has to *wait*: the F8 executor publishes `afterCommit` in a `REQUIRES_NEW` transaction, so reading `fire_count` once right after the HTTP response reports a rule that did fire as not-fired. My first probe run "failed" on exactly that and I nearly recorded a working control as broken |



## F2-101 · The authorisation matrix demands a CAB and the code lets direct votes stand in

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-101 *(found by S7b, measuring what "the CAB queue" actually means before building an agenda over it)* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` — `ChangeManagementService#requireConfiguredChangeAuthority` + the `CAB_APPROVED` transition |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the column that records which body may authorise a change does not decide which body does |
| **Current State** | `change_authorisation_matrix.gate` names the authorising body per (change type × risk): measured **15 CAB + 18 ECAB + 3 AUTO of 36 rows**, identical in dev and prod. The `CAB_APPROVED` transition reads it, and if the gate is CAB/ECAB it checks only that the gate is *configurable* — `gateConfigurationFault` — then, absent a referral, falls through to the **legacy recorded-votes path** (`change_approvals`: ≥ `cabMinApprovals` APPROVE, zero REJECT). Referral is a separate explicit call (`POST /{id}/refer-to-committee`), never a consequence of the transition |
| **Measured** | **8 changes in dev and 8 in prod** are `CAB_APPROVED` or beyond, their matrix row demands **CAB or ECAB**, and they have **no committee referral at all**. And of the changes awaiting a decision right now, **3 of 4 in dev and 3 of 3 in prod** have no referral either |
| **Problem** | "Approved by a quorate committee" and "approved by some people who voted" are not the same governance fact, and the difference is exactly what a SAMA or NCA examiner asks about. The committee path carries an active-roster denominator, COI and SoD recusal, a chair clause, e-signed minutes over a content hash and — since F2-90 — a ledger entry. The direct path carries named approval rows. The matrix column that is supposed to say which one is required is **advisory**, which is the same defect [[F2-95]] found one column to the left in the same table, one slice earlier |
| **What it is NOT** | Not an ungated bypass. The direct path has its own gate (CM-C2: a REJECT on record blocks approval, and `cabMinApprovals` APPROVEs are required), and `requireConfiguredChangeAuthority` still fails closed when the tenant's gate map is broken (A17-D2/F2-96). The `AUTO` rows demand nothing **by design** and are correct. So this is a wrong-authority finding, not a no-authority one |
| **Recommendation — and it is an OWNER decision** | Making `gate=CAB` require a referral is a one-line change and it would **refuse the path by which 8 changes in each database were authorised**, plus every future change in a tenant that does not use committees. That is S6b-ii's shape exactly, and the same three options apply: (a) hard gate; (b) report the divergence and leave the path open; (c) per-tenant opt-in, default off — the V348 pattern. The measurement is what the decision needs and it is now on the record; picking (a) without it would have been the control-refuses-everything mistake for the third time |
| **Status** | ⬜ **Open — measured, filed, and deliberately not enforced in S7b.** S7b's own scope was the agenda; enforcing the gate changes who may authorise a change, which is not a decision to take inside a slice about agenda lines |
| **References** | `ChangeManagementService#requireConfiguredChangeAuthority` · the `CAB_APPROVED` case · `ChangeAuthorisationMatrix#gate` · [[F2-95]] · [[F2-96]] · [[F2-102]] |
| **Lesson** | **A column that names an authority is not an authority.** Two slices running, the same table has produced the same defect: `required_roles` was advisory (F2-95) and `gate` is advisory (F2-101). Worth checking every remaining column of `change_authorisation_matrix` against its enforcement rather than waiting for the third one to surface |


## F2-102 · A committee referral outlives the record it refers to, and nothing notices

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-102 *(found by S7b while defining the CAB queue — it is why the queue has a presence test)* |
| **Module** | `admin/committee` — `committee_referral` |
| **Severity** | **P3** — dev-only debris today, but it is the input an agenda builder trusts |
| **Current State** | Measured 2026-08-03 in dev: the only two `PENDING` referrals, **#7 and #8**, both created by `compliance`, point at `source_entity_id` **101** and **102** — and **no `change_requests` row with either id exists in any organisation**. Deleting a change does not withdraw, delete or flag its referral, and no sweep or report detects the dangling pair. Prod holds zero referrals, so this is dev debris rather than a production data problem |
| **Problem** | The referral table is what the CAB queue is read from. A naive agenda keyed on `status = 'PENDING'` would have listed **two items that do not exist while omitting all three real ones** — wrong in both directions, which is [[F2-96]]'s shape: a confident report that is wrong twice over. Governance artefacts that name non-existent items are worse than an empty agenda, because an empty agenda is visibly empty |
| **Fix (partial, in S7b)** | The queue read requires V350's subject snapshot (`source_code`), so pre-V350 rows — including both orphans — are excluded **for a stated reason rather than by luck**, and `CommitteeReferralGateTest.theQueueExcludesSubjectlessReferrals` pins it with the measurement in the test body. That makes the agenda correct; it does **not** clean up or detect the orphans |
| **Still open** | Nothing withdraws a referral when its source record is deleted, and nothing reports a dangling one. The clean fix needs a direction the boundary has no port for (the committee module cannot ask whether a `change_requests` row exists — ADR-012, and S5a took those imports to zero), so it belongs on the **referring** side: the change module's delete path should withdraw the referral, and a health check should count danglers |
| **Status** | ⬜ **Open** — the agenda is protected; the orphans are not cleaned. Filed rather than swept, because a `DELETE` against governance-routing rows needs the owner's explicit OK (house rule 6) |
| **References** | `CommitteeReferralService#awaitingDecision` · `V350` · [[F2-96]] · [[F2-101]] |
| **Lesson** | **A queue is only as trustworthy as what removes things from it.** Every control in this module reads the referral row — the gate, the recusal list, the decision write-back — and none of them asks whether the thing referred still exists. ⚠ And a practical one: **the orphans are almost certainly my own probe debris from an earlier slice**, which is the argument for cleanup on the referring side rather than a manual delete: a probe that leaves routing rows behind will do it again.


## F2-103 · The CAB approval recorded when it was decided and never who

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-103 *(found by S7c while adding two decision branches next to the existing ones)* |
| **Module** | `change_mgmt` — `ChangeManagementService`, the `CAB_APPROVED` transition |
| **Severity** | **P2** — the platform's central authorisation act had a timestamp and an anonymous approver |
| **Current State** | `stampDecisionAuthor` had exactly **two** call sites: `EMERGENCY_APPROVED` and `CAB_REJECTED`. **Not** `CAB_APPROVED`. So the ordinary CAB approval set `cab_decision_at` and `cab_decision_notes` and left `cab_decision_by_id` / `cab_decision_by_name` NULL |
| **Measured** | **Zero** rows in dev carry `cab_decision_by_id`, and **2** carry `cab_decision_at` without it. The only test asserting the column lives in `EmergencyRatificationTest`. (In prod the columns do not exist yet — V345 is among the pending set) |
| **Problem** | This is [[F2-93]]'s own finding, unfixed on the branch that matters most. F2-93 was raised because "the record says when the CAB decided and never who" and was fixed **while A17-D3 was in flight**, so the author landed on the two paths that slice touched. The SoD check F2-93 existed to enable — comparing the approver against the requester/owner — had nothing to compare against for an approval, which is the only decision where that comparison matters |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a missing column (V345 added both) and not a missing audit trail: the ledger's `TRANSITION` row names the actor. But an audit row and the record are two different reads, and every report, export and board pack reads the record |
| **Fix** | One line — `stampDecisionAuthor(c)` in the `CAB_APPROVED` branch — plus `CabDecisionOutcomesTest.everyDecisionBranchStampsItsAuthor`, which parses the real switch and fails if **any** `DECISION_TARGETS` branch records a decision without its author or without `cab_decision_at`. Mutation-proven: restoring the omission fails that test |
| **Status** | ✅ **Fixed 2026-08-03** (no migration — the columns already existed). Live: an ordinary approval now stamps `cab_decision_by_id=3` / `cab_decision_by_name=compliance` where every previous row had NULL |
| **References** | `ChangeManagementService` `CAB_APPROVED` branch · `#stampDecisionAuthor` · `CabDecisionOutcomesTest` · [[F2-93]] · [[F2-90]] |
| **Lesson** | **A fix applied while working on one path stops at the edge of that path.** F2-93 was correct, tested and incomplete, and the incompleteness was invisible because its test lived in the slice that found it. When a fix adds a field that several branches should set, the guard has to be *"every branch of this kind sets it"* — a per-branch assertion cannot fail for the branch nobody wrote it for. Same shape as [[F2-100]], where one publish site was mistaken for the only one needed |


## F2-104 · The audit ledger's structured status columns are used by 4 of 32 transition types

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-104 *(found by S7c's live probe asserting the wrong column, which is how the gap surfaced)* |
| **Module** | `platform/auditlog` — `platform.audit_log.workflow_status_before` / `_after` |
| **Severity** | **P3** — the history is recorded and readable, but only queryable by `LIKE` over prose |
| **Current State** | Measured 2026-08-03 in dev: **18 of 7,701** audit rows populate `workflow_status_after`, and they come from **4 of the 32** entity types that record a `TRANSITION` action (all four are `Bcm*`). Every other transition — including every `ChangeRequest` one — puts the statuses in the free-text `message`: *"Change CHG-2025-0014 status CAB_PENDING → CAB_APPROVED"* |
| **Problem** | The columns exist to make lifecycle history *queryable*. "Show me every change that reached `CAB_APPROVED` last quarter, and who moved it" is a one-line query against the structured columns and a `LIKE '%status%→ CAB_APPROVED%'` against prose — which breaks the moment a message is reworded, and cannot be indexed. The tamper-evident chain (F2-31) hashes the row either way, so nothing is *lost*; it is a findability defect, not an integrity one |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a change-module defect and not caused by S7c: it is platform-wide and pre-dates every slice in this programme. And not a silent one — the information is in the message, so an auditor reading the ledger sees the transition |
| **Recommendation** | Populate the two columns from the transition call sites. Cheap per site, but there are **28 entity types** to touch, so it is a sweep with a ratchet (the `AuditLogService` overload that takes before/after already exists — the four `Bcm*` types use it), not a one-line fix. Sequence it after S7/S8 rather than inside a slice about CAB outcomes |
| **Status** | ⬜ **Open — filed, not fixed.** S7c's probe was repointed to read the message, with a comment saying why, rather than quietly asserting a weaker thing |
| **References** | `platform.audit_log` · `AuditLogService` · [[F2-31]] (the hash chain, which is unaffected) · [[F2-90]] |
| **Lesson** | **A probe that asserts the wrong column finds a real gap.** My assertion read `workflow_status_after`, failed, and the honest answer was neither "my probe is wrong" nor "the code is broken" but "these columns are populated by 4 of 32 producers". ⚠ Also worth noting the shape: a nullable column that *one* module fills in looks like a feature and behaves like a convention

## F2-105 · The post-implementation review card could not save, and that is why the retrospective looked unused

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-105 *(found by S7d's live probe — three legs 400'd on their first run)* |
| **Module** | `tss-grc-ui-v2` — `pages/change/ChangePirCard.tsx` against `PUT /api/v1/changes/{id}` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — a whole governance feature was unusable, and its emptiness read as "nobody uses this" |
| **Current State** | ✅ **FIXED 2026-08-04** (S7d). The card now sends `{...change, …pirFields}`, the shape the register's edit form has always used |
| **Problem** | `PUT /api/v1/changes/{id}` binds the `ChangeRequest` **entity** with `@Valid`, and [[F2-30]] put `@NotBlank` on `titleAr` ("Arabic names are backend-mandatory" — asserted in CLAUDE.md and enforced nowhere until then). The server's `apply()` is a deliberate *partial merge* that skips null fields, but `@Valid` runs **before** it, so a body carrying only the four PIR fields is rejected 400 with `errors: {titleAr: "must not be blank"}` and `apply()` is never reached. `PirCard.save()` sent exactly those four fields. So **the PIR card had been unsaveable since F2-30 landed** — every "Save review" click produced a bare validation toast |
| **How it was found** | S7d set out to consume `pir_follow_up`, having measured **zero** populated PIR fields across dev and prod and concluded the field went nowhere. The live probe's `PUT` legs then 400'd, and the sharper truth appeared: the column was not unused, it was **unreachable**. The measurement that opened the slice had been reading the *consequence* of this defect and attributing it to indifference |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a server defect and not a reason to relax the server. Dropping `@Valid` from the PUT, or moving `@NotBlank` off `titleAr`, would reverse F2-30 — bilingual records are a product requirement (house rule 7) and the constraint was measured safe (0 blank rows) before it landed. A partial-merge API with required fields legitimately demands the caller resend them, and the register's edit form always did |
| **Fix** | `ChangePirCard.save()` spreads the record. `status` is safe to resend because `apply()` deliberately never copies it ([[CM-C3]]). Pinned by `pirCardSavePayload.test.ts`, which reads the **entity's own** bean-validation annotations rather than hard-coding `titleAr`, so a future `@NotBlank` on another column cannot quietly break the card again — mutation-proven by removing the spread |
| **Blast radius, measured** | `changeApi.update` has three call sites. The register's edit form sends `{...c}` (`reqFromDto`) and worked; `updateTask` is a different endpoint; the PIR card was the only partial sender. No other module's card was affected — this is a change-module instance of a general trap, not a platform-wide sweep |
| **References** | `ChangePirCard.tsx` · `ChangeManagementController.update` · `ChangeRequest.titleAr` · [[F2-30]] (the constraint that exposed it) · [[F2-98]] (the same module's other honesty defect) |
| **Lesson** | **"Nothing consumes this column" and "nothing can write this column" produce identical data.** Zero populated rows was the finding I built a slice on, and it had two possible causes; I checked the read side and inferred the write side. ⚠ And the general trap: **adding a constraint to an entity that doubles as a partial-update DTO breaks every caller that was legitimately sending a subset** — `@Valid` on a merge endpoint validates the *patch* as if it were the whole record, and there is nothing at the point of the annotation to warn you |

## F2-106 · The evidence subsystem accepts a link to any module name and any evidence id, checking neither

|  |  |
|---|---|
| **ID** | F2-106 *(found by S8a while writing the change module's adapter over the peer)* |
| **Module** | `module/evidence` — `EvidenceItemService.addLink` / `EvidenceLinkRequest` |
| **Severity** | **P2** — two distinct defects on one endpoint: an unvalidated vocabulary that surfaces as a 500, and an unvalidated foreign key that writes a cross-tenant reference |
| **Current State** | ⬜ **OPEN.** S8a defends the change module's own side (`EvidenceAdapter` resolves the item in-tenant before linking, and `EvidenceAdapterTest` regression-tests it), but the peer's endpoint is untouched — every other caller, and any future one, is still exposed |
| **Problem (a) — the vocabulary** | `EvidenceLinkRequest.linkedModule` carries `@NotBlank` and nothing else, while the column is guarded by `CK_ev_links_module` (**9** permitted values before V353, 10 after). A caller sending any other string — a typo, a lower-case `risk`, a module that has not joined the subsystem — gets a **500** out of a constraint violation instead of a 400 naming the permitted set. This is the `ck_ca_source` landmine ([[green-finance-rbac-impl-progress]]) in a second place: a closed database vocabulary with no application-layer counterpart |
| **Problem (b) — the tenant** | `evidenceId` carries `@NotNull` and nothing else. `addLink` never confirms the item **exists**, and never confirms it belongs to the caller's organisation, so a link row pointing at another tenant's evidence item persists successfully. The read side filters the *link's* `organisation_id`, so the practical effect is a silently dead reference rather than a disclosure — but "the reference is dangling" and "the reference is to somebody else's evidence" are indistinguishable in the row, and a future read that joins on `evidence_id` without re-filtering would disclose it. Measured: `attached()` in S8a's adapter re-filters the **item** for exactly this reason |
| **How it was found** | Not by probing the evidence module. S8a needed `attach()` to promise "an existing evidence item", so the adapter resolved the id before delegating — and reading `addLink` to decide whether that resolution was redundant showed it was the only place doing it |
| **What it is NOT** | Not a reason to add validation in the adapter *instead* of the service — the adapter's check protects change and nothing else. Not an argument for a `@Pattern` on `linkedModule`: the vocabulary lives in the CHECK and a duplicated regex is the F2-91 shape (a vocabulary stated in N places was wrong in N−1 of them). The right fix reads the permitted set from one place, or an enum the migration is ratcheted against |
| **Fix, proposed** | (1) A `LinkedModule` enum or constant set in `module/evidence`, ratcheted against `CK_ev_links_module` by a test that parses the migration — the same instrument `EvidenceAdapterTest.theModuleStringMatchesTheMigration` uses for one value. (2) `addLink` resolves the evidence item by id **and** organisation, 404 when it does not. (3) A backfill audit: count existing `evidence_links` whose `evidence_id` resolves in a different organisation, or in none — S8a measured **21** links total across 8 modules, so this is a small, checkable set |
| **References** | `EvidenceItemService.addLink` · `EvidenceLinkRequest` · `V353__evidence_link_change_module.sql` · `module/change_adapters/EvidenceAdapter.java` (the defended side) · [[F2-91]] (the same duplicated-vocabulary shape) · [[F2-94]] (a closed vocabulary plus a migration ratchet, as the pattern to copy) |
| **Lesson** | **A database CHECK is not input validation, and the difference is a 500 instead of a 400.** Every closed vocabulary in the schema needs an application-layer counterpart *and* a test binding the two — which is what F2-94 built for escalation recipient keys and what `ck_ca_source` still lacks. Second: **writing an adapter over a peer is a free audit of that peer**, because a port's contract forces you to ask what the far side actually guarantees |

## Sequencing — what to do, in what order

Dependency-ordered, not severity-ordered. Severity says what matters; this says what is *possible*.

| Wave | Items | Why this order |
|---|---|---|
| **First (this week, ahead of everything)** | **F2-21 vote provenance** · F2-22 audit script + `@JsonIgnore` mitigation | F2-21 invalidates the platform's central control claim and takes ~2 days. Nothing else in this register earns more per hour. F2-22 rides alongside because it is the same question — *who is allowed to set this field?* |
| **Now (days)** | F2-10 restart · F2-02 three files · F2-07 reconciliation matrix · F2-08a export-toolkit promotion · F2-08b boundary ratchet · F2-19 CI-generated baseline · F2-20 delete | Highest value per hour. Each is small, independent, and needs no decision. |
| **Wave 1 (weeks)** | F2-01 module registry → then F2-03 test floors, F2-05 permission conversion, F2-06 enforcement test | The registry is the spine: floors, permissions, schemas and navigation all attach to it. Nothing durable is buildable before it. |
| **Wave 2** | F2-04 entitlement **rollout** (**gated on the per-module SKU decision**) · F2-09 schema convergence · F2-13 grant tightness · F2-14 control-health SPI | Wave 2 is where the modular business model becomes real — and where the platform's assurance story becomes saleable. F2-04 is a rollout of an existing subsystem, so it is days rather than weeks. |
| **Wave 3** | F2-07 workspace IA · F2-11 i18n convergence · F2-12 `ndi` · F2-16 monoliths · F2-15 remaining mocks · F2-17 performance · F2-18 integration tests | Larger, benefits from Waves 1–2 being in place (IA needs entitlement; `ndi` needs test floors). |

**The one decision that must be made before Wave 2:** is the commercial model **per-module SKUs** or a
single suite? F2-04, F2-05 and F2-06 are P0/P1 under the first reading and P2 hygiene under the second.
This is Baseline §9 question 2, and it is a business decision — it is not ours to assume.
